## <a id="d-105"></a>D-105 · 2026-11-25 — Safety rules read curly punctuation as straight; Exp 24's safety step is redone and the started vote is voided (user)
Decided by the user, 2026-11-25, after about 5 of 29 prompts had been voted on.

**Bug.** The name rule (`_named_person` in `backend/app/safety.py`) knows `I've`, `I'm` and similar only with a straight apostrophe. Written with a curly one (`I’ve`), the contraction read as a capitalised unknown word, so as a name, and the line was rejected. Only qwen3:4b-instruct writes curly apostrophes. In the Exp 24 run: 412 of qwen's 900 lines had one, **203 (23%) were rejected as a named person on a contraction, and 81 of its 180 threads were lost to that alone**; bonsai and Haiku had none. The ballot shows one playable thread per model, so qwen was often shown a weaker thread, or none (p19, a teens prompt, had no playable qwen thread). The vote would have judged the filter, not the model. A live show on qwen would have lost about a quarter of its lines to the bank. Caught while checking why the ballot had 29 prompts, not 30.

**Fix.** The rules read curly apostrophes and quotes as straight ones (`CURLY`, applied at the top of the rules); the LLM layer still sees the line as written. `RULES_VERSION` is now `rules-v2`, and each Exp 24 attempt records the rules version that judged it, so a run redoes only the safety step for bits judged by older rules (generation is never repeated). All 180 bits are re-judged, for every model, so every candidate is judged by the same rules: about 180 Haiku calls, about $0.02.

**Exp 19 is unaffected, by construction and by test.** The red-team list, the clean list and the bank have no curly characters; a test writes every one of their lines curly and checks the rule verdict is unchanged, adults and teens. Removing the fix makes the new tests fail.

**The started vote is voided.** About 5 prompts had been ranked; qwen's shown thread can change after the re-check, so those rankings may have compared other bits. The ballot, key and votes are archived (gitignored) and rebuilt; the user votes again from the start. That costs about 5 prompts of the user's time.

**Not changed.** "That's" capitalised mid-sentence is still rejected as a possible name, straight or curly: that is the rule working as designed, and it applies to every model alike.

## <a id="d-106"></a>D-106 · 2026-11-25 — Exp 24's ballot and judge show punctuation in plain form, so no model is given away (user)
Decided by the user, 2026-11-25, before the restarted vote.

**Why.** In the Exp 24 run only qwen3:4b-instruct writes typographic punctuation: of its 900 lines, 412 have a curly apostrophe, 17 a curly opening quote, 158 an em dash and 35 an ellipsis; bonsai (885 lines) and Haiku (870) have none of these. Shown as written, any of them tells the voter, or the D-95 judge, which bits are qwen's, and the vote is no longer blind. The judge's model-bias check exists for exactly this; removing the tell is cheaper than catching it.

**Choice.** `sim/blind.py` maps `’ ‘` to `'`, `“ ”` to `"`, `—` to ` - `, `–` to `-` and `…` to `...` for display, in the ballot and in the judge's prompt. Stored bits keep what each model wrote, so the run's data and the safety verdicts are unchanged. Tests: no tell survives on a real export, and removing the mapping makes the test fail.

**Not hidden, said plainly.** Style beyond punctuation (word choice, line length, how a bit builds) can still hint at a model; that is part of what is being judged.


## <a id="d-107"></a>D-107 · 2026-11-25 — Exp 24: bonsai-2:4b writes the live show's bits and is the SFT/DPO base; Haiku is the funnier reference to beat (user)
Decided by the user, 2026-11-25, after seeing the score. The rule was fixed before any run (D-94, D-101); the score is `python -m sim.compare_generators score`, stored in `reports/metrics/model_comparison.json` (numbers only, no joke text).

**Result.** 60 prompts per model, one attempt each; 30 prompts ranked by the user, 0 by the judge (the D-95 judge was not used). All three models are eligible (p95 within 12 s, failure upper bound within 15%, local models 100% on the GPU).

| Model | Attempts | Failed (95% upper) | p50 | p95 | Line pass | Playable | Mean rank (95% CI) | Cost, 5 shows | GPU | Eligible |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| qwen3-4b-instruct | 60 | 0 (6%) | 6.7 s | 8.2 s | 87% | 87% | 2.17 (1.90 to 2.43) | $0.000 | 100% | yes |
| bonsai-2-4b | 60 | 1 (9%) | 5.2 s | 6.9 s | 89% | 92% | 2.17 (1.87 to 2.43) | $0.000 | 100% | yes |
| claude-haiku-5-5 | 60 | 2 (11%) | 5.6 s | 7.4 s | 91% | 95% | 1.67 (1.40 to 1.97) | $0.017 | - | yes |

**Pick by the rule: bonsai-2-4b.** Funniness is inconclusive between claude-haiku-5-5 and bonsai-2-4b (the paired CI includes 0), so the cheaper, then faster, of the two.

**Funniness numbers.** Haiku vs bonsai, paired rank gap: 0.50 in Haiku's favour, 95% CI 0.00 to 1.00. The bar is a lower bound above 0, so it is not clear: it misses by touching 0. First places: Haiku 16 of 30, bonsai 8, qwen 6. Ranked last: qwen 3, bonsai 4, Haiku 2 (a 3-way ranking, so these are small counts). Head to head, restricted to bonsai and qwen: qwen is 0.13 ranks ahead (mean rank 1.43 vs 1.57), CI -0.20 to 0.47, so inconclusive.

**Tie-break: the rule's pick between the two local models was alphabetical.** qwen and bonsai tie exactly on mean rank among the eligible three (2.17). `decide()` sorts by (mean rank, name), so bonsai, not qwen, became the "second" model compared with Haiku. Had the tie broken the other way, the same cheaper-then-faster step would have picked qwen ($0 against Haiku's $0.017). So the vote does not separate the two local models: head to head qwen is 0.13 ranks ahead, CI -0.20 to 0.47. What does separate them favours bonsai: p50 5.2 s vs 6.7 s, p95 6.9 s vs 8.2 s, and playable 92% vs 87%. The user's choice below keeps bonsai on those grounds, not on the name order. **Follow-up, built in D-108:** an exact tie in mean rank now goes to the faster model. It gives the same result here.

**Options offered.** (a) Override the rule and pick Haiku by judgment: the user ranked it first most often and finds it funnier. (b) Rank the other 30 prompts first. (c, chosen) Keep bonsai-2-4b as the rule says.

**Choice (c), the user's reason:** it gives a good baseline to improve. Haiku has no weights, so it cannot be fine-tuned; the SFT/DPO base had to be local whatever the vote said. Haiku stays the funnier reference: its 0.5-rank lead over bonsai is the gap SFT and DPO must close.

**Why not (b).** Ranking more prompts after seeing the result is a stopping rule that depends on the data. It inflates false "clear" results, and the bar was fixed at 30 prompts before any vote (D-94's power estimate). The 30 are the result.

**Next.** The 120-pair ranking tool (D-102) is built on bonsai. Wiring bonsai into the show as `JOKE_WRITER` (Ollama provider, reply schema, reasoning off) is a separate PR. The live show still runs on Gemini until then.

**What would change it.** A trained bonsai that does not beat bonsai-2-4b as it is on the pairs the user ranks (D-102). Or a live show where bonsai misses the 12 s deadline when it shares the GPU with Kokoro: the speeds above were measured without the voice, so they are a best case (D-97). Then the fallback writer is Haiku ($0.017 for 5 shows).

**Judgment calls, not measured.** That the cheap-then-fast tie-break is the right way to pick between two models whose funniness is a tie. That 30 ranked prompts, one rater, is enough: the user is one taste, and the room's taste is not measured here. Playable and line-pass rates come from the Haiku safety filter and its rules, which judge qwen's, bonsai's and Haiku's lines by the same rules (D-105).


## <a id="d-108"></a>D-108 · 2026-11-25 — Exp 24: an exact tie in mean rank goes to the faster model, not the first name (user)
Decided by the user, 2026-11-25, after D-107 showed the gap. Built in the same PR as D-107.

**Problem.** `decide()` sorted eligible models by (mean rank, name) to find the best and the second. In Exp 24, qwen and bonsai tied exactly at 2.17, so bonsai went on to the Haiku comparison because "bonsai" sorts before "qwen". A name says nothing about a model; had the names sorted the other way, the rule would have picked qwen.

**Options.** (a) Keep the name order: harmless today, arbitrary next time. (b) **Break an exact tie on p50 latency, then name** (user). (c) Break it on cost: both local models cost $0, so it would fall back to the name anyway. (d) Treat a tie as a tie and run the cheaper-then-faster step on all tied models: more code for the same outcome here.

**Choice (b).** The sort key is (mean rank, p50, name). Speed is already the rule's second criterion after cost (D-94), and it is what separates the tied pair here.

**Effect on Exp 24: none.** bonsai's p50 is 5.2 s and qwen's 6.7 s, so bonsai is still the second model and the pick is unchanged (D-107; the stored metrics and the notebook checks are unchanged). It matters only for later comparisons that tie exactly, such as trained vs base bonsai.

**Changing a pre-fixed rule after a result.** Allowed here because it does not change this result, and the change is the same rule's own next criterion, not a new one. Test: two models with equal mean rank, the slower one first by name; the faster goes through (`tests/test_compare_generators.py`), and it fails on the old key.

**Judgment call, not measured.** No experiment: a rule for ties, with no data that would favour another order.

## <a id="d-109"></a>D-109 · 2026-11-25 — bonsai-2:4b writes the show through Ollama; each show loads it first and refuses to start unless it is all on the GPU (user)
Decided by the user, 2026-11-25, while wiring D-107's pick into the show.

**What ships.** `JOKE_WRITER_PROVIDER=ollama` (new; `bonsai-2:4b` by default, `OLLAMA_URL` to move the server, no key) builds the same OpenAI-compatible client Exp 24 timed: thinking off (`reasoning_effort: none`), the reply schema sent as `json_schema`, the generator's 10 s attempt / 12 s deadline and 2 retries. A test holds the show's client to the harness's `make_client` field by field, so the show runs what was measured. `backend/sample.env` ships it.

**Warm-up (judgment call, not measured).** A local model's first request loads it into VRAM: Exp 24's warm-ups took 6.1 s and 0.8 s for bonsai, and Ollama drops an idle model after 5 minutes by default. Inside a bit that eats the 12 s deadline, so each show sends one untimed request (300 s budget, no retries, on a copy of the client so the show's counters are untouched) before the microphone and the log open. `OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=-1` in the run command keeps it loaded between shows. **Options:** (a) no warm-up: the first bit of each show risks the bank; (b) warm at server start: a model dropped while idle would still reload mid-show; (c, chosen) warm at each show's start.

**The GPU check (user's choice).** The first real smoke run (2026-11-25) loaded bonsai in 19.3 s and then timed out both bits at 12 s: `gemma4:12b` was loaded beside it at context 32768, holding 6.8 of 8 GB and 85% of the GPU, so bonsai was not listed in `/api/ps` as on the GPU. The warm-up had passed, so a show would have played every bit from the bank. **Options:** (a, chosen) refuse the show: after the warm-up, read `/api/ps`; unless the writer is listed with all of it in VRAM, the show stops before `show_start` with an error naming what else is loaded (`ollama stop <name>`); (b) warn and run: the bank covers the bits, but the show is not what was measured; (c) no check. It is D-97's gate, simplified for the show: 100% on the GPU and loaded at context 8192 (the context Exp 24 measured; a server started without `OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH`, or by the tray app, loads at another one), no file-size check, and a missing, implausible or unreadable report counts as not on the GPU (fails closed). Names are compared as Ollama lists them, so a model given without a tag matches `<name>:latest`. Added in review.

**Smoke run once the GPU was free (2026-11-25, same server):** warm-up 10.5 s; bonsai 100% on the GPU at context 8192; six bits, one per mode, in 4.6 to 6.1 s, no retries, no bad replies (Exp 24's p50: 5.2 s). No safety check or voice in this run; Exp 23's shows cover those.

**A failed warm-up stops the show before it starts.** The browser gets `show failed: <why>` and no rating prompt; no session log is written, so no bank-only show is logged as a bonsai show. The operator fixes Ollama and presses Start again.

**What would change it.** Exp 23's simulated shows (between-bit silence with the LLM stack) run on bonsai now; a gap over its bar would mean the 5.2 s p50 does not hold beside Kokoro.


## <a id="d-110"></a>D-110 · 2026-11-25 — Generator: one thread per call, three calls in parallel on one prompt (Exp 26 checks the timing) (user)
Decided by the user, 2026-11-25. **Problem:** bonsai-2:4b (the live writer, D-107, D-109) will be trained with SFT and DPO. The user picked the DPO format: **one training example is one bit request and one thread of 5 jokes.** A DPO pair is then two separate samples on the same prompt, the user's label covers exactly the 5 jokes the audience hears, SFT data has the same shape, and the base and trained bonsai are compared in the same format. gen-v3 wrote 3 threads in one reply, which fits none of that.

| Option | For | Against | Choice |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) Whole-reply DPO: keep 3 threads in one reply, prefer one whole reply | No change to the generator | The label covers 15 jokes of which the show plays 5, so it is noisy; a reply is 3x as long to read and to train on | Rejected by the user |
| (c) Keep 3 threads per reply and prefer one thread inside it | No change to the generator | Not standard DPO: two samples of a prompt are not what is compared, so it needs a custom trainer | Rejected by the user |
| (b1) One thread per call, 3 calls in sequence, each told the premises already drafted for this bit (`premises_this_bit`) | Premises stay distinct | About 3x slower than parallel; the calls are not independent samples of one prompt, so it is not DPO's sampling distribution | Rejected by the user |
| **(b2) One thread per call, 3 calls in parallel on the identical prompt** | Exactly DPO's pair distribution (independent samples of one prompt); faster than (b1) | Cost: 3 calls per bit instead of 1; two threads of a bit may share a premise | **Chosen by the user** |

**What changed.** `app.generate` is now gen-v4. `ThreadGenerator` makes `K = 3` calls per bit at once on a small thread pool; each call gets the same system and user message (`venue`, `energy`, `premises_already_told`, as in v3: only the premises of threads the show played) and returns `{"premise": str, "jokes": [5 strings]}`, sent as `json_reply` with the matching schema, at temperature 0.9. `premises_this_bit` and its instruction are gone. The `Batch` the show reads is unchanged in shape: joke-major lines over the threads that came back, in call order (not completion order), with premises aligned to threads. `played_thread` bookkeeping is as before.

**Failure and time.** One 12 s budget (`GENERATOR_TIMING[1]`, the number the bridge lines are sized for) covers the whole bit. The calls start together, so each gets the full budget as its `deadline_s` (a new optional per-call override on both clients' `chat`, so the client's counters and Exp 20's `timed()` wrapper stay on the one client). A call that fails, times out or returns a bad shape drops only its thread. If no thread came back, the bit raises `GenerationError` for every joke with no further calls, and the show falls back to the bank, as before. No call starts with less than `llm.MIN_ATTEMPT_S` left. `show._pick_thread` already reads the thread count from the batch, so a bit with 1 or 2 threads plays one thread (tests cover 1 and 2). A bit with fewer threads shows fewer items per `candidates` row, and `threads_lost` says why (see Logging below).

**Counters.** The client's counters (`attempts`, `retried`, `failed`, `bad_replies`, tokens) are now touched from several threads, so they are updated under a lock (`llm.bump`); a test hammers one client from 16 threads and checks the totals. Exp 20's `timed()` appends to a list, which is safe in CPython, and now records 3 generator calls per bit.

**Retries and hosted writers.** Judgment call, not measured: with a 10 s attempt inside a 12 s budget, a retry after a first-attempt timeout gets about 1.5 s, so retries rarely help the local writer (Exp 26's failure reasons will show it). A hosted writer (Gemini, Claude) now sends 3 requests at once per bit, so a free-tier rate limit is reached three times faster than with one call; Exp 20's quota count is the old one-call figure until it is rerun on gen-v4.

**Logging (additive schema change).** A dropped thread must leave a trace (invariant 6). Each `candidates` row now has `threads_lost`: a list with one short reason per dropped call (error class and message, for example `LLMError: transport: ReadTimeout`, never joke text), empty when every call succeeded. When no thread came back, the reasons are on every joke's row of that bit, next to the bank items. It is optional on read, so older logs validate unchanged, and the schema number stays 2. Documented in docs/plans/live-loop.md.

**Ollama.** Parallel requests to one model use Ollama's `OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL` slots, and each slot has its own KV cache for its context, so VRAM use rises with the number of slots. The D-109 check (100% on the GPU, context 8192) must still pass with 3 slots: the run command now sets `OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL=3` (backend/README.md, backend/sample.env). **Measured 2026-11-25** on the user's laptop (RTX 4060, 8 GB) with `OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH=8192 OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=-1 OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL=3`, through `generate.warm_up` and `generate.placement`: warm-up 14.1 s; bonsai-2:4b 100% on the GPU; `/api/ps` reports context 8192 (per slot, not 3 x 8192, so D-109's context rule holds unchanged); 2.58 GB in VRAM against 1.94 GB with one slot (about 0.64 GB for the two extra slots); GPU memory used 2.9 of 8.2 GB; keep-alive -1 keeps it loaded. So the slots fit. This does not show that Ollama runs the 3 calls concurrently: Exp 26's queueing ratio measures that, and it re-reads the placement after the run.

**Exp 24 is frozen on gen-v3.** Its stored run (`data/model_comparison/gen-2a512264/`) was made with the one-call generator, and `score` asserts the digest. The v3 generator, prompt, schema and constants moved unchanged into `backend/app/generate_v3.py`; `sim/compare_generators.py` and `sim/screen_local.py` import that, and a test asserts the digest is still `2a512264`. Exp 24's pick (D-107) therefore stands as measured; it says nothing about gen-v4's quality. Exp 20 and the show use gen-v4, so Exp 20's cache key changes, which is correct.

**Exp 26 (the check that decides whether this ships as it is).** `sim/thread_timing.py`, notebook section "Exp 26 Per-thread timing". 20 bits on the show's own writer client after the warm-up, on 20 seeded prompts that are not Exp 24's, no filter, no voice. **Bar, fixed before any run:** at most 1 of 20 bits loses a thread to a timeout or a failure; no bit has zero threads; the writer is still 100% on the GPU at context 8192 after the run. **How queueing shows.** The calls are timed at the client, which includes Ollama's queue wait. With one slot (`OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL=1`) the three calls last about 1x, 2x and 3x one call, so the slowest is about 3 times the fastest, and the bit's wall time is the longest of them either way. Run in parallel they last about the same, ratio near 1. Wall time against the sum of the calls cannot tell these apart, so Exp 26 reports the ratio. **Reported beside it, no bar:** bit wall time p50 and p95, per-call p50 and p95 next to Exp 24's one-call p50 (5.2 s), how many bits queued (the slowest call at least twice the fastest), and how many bits have two delivered threads with the same premise (case-insensitive exact match). **The number that changes the decision:** more than 1 lost bit, any empty bit, or the writer off the GPU. **Then:** 2 threads per bit, or a longer budget with more bridge lines, or fewer slots: a new decision. Not run yet; the user runs it.

**Not measured.** That three samples of one prompt differ enough to give the filter and the selector a real choice (Exp 26 counts same-premise bits, with no bar); that parallel calls leave each reply as good as one call's (the 120-pair ranking tool on gen-v4 is where that shows up).
**Result, Exp 26 (2026-11-25, the user's run, `reports/metrics/thread_timing.json`): passed.** 20 bits, 0 lost a thread (bar: at most 1), 0 bits with no thread, the writer 100% on the GPU at context 8192 after the run. Beside the bar: bit wall time p50 3.53 s, p95 5.12 s (inside the 12 s budget with room); call p50 3.17 s, p95 4.77 s (Exp 24's one-call p50 was 5.16 s); 1 of 20 bits queued, median slowest-to-fastest ratio 1.16, so Ollama ran the three calls at the same time; 0 bits with two threads on one premise; 0 bits re-asked. So 3 parallel calls fit the bit's budget and D-110 ships as it is. **Not exercised:** `bad_replies` was 0, so the re-ask path (D-111) never ran in this record and "Ollama enforces the counted schema" stays a judgment call, not measured: no bad shape appeared, which fits the schema holding but cannot show it.


## <a id="d-111"></a>D-111 · 2026-11-25 — Generator: count the jokes in the schema, re-ask a bad shape, and test a connected prompt (Exp 27 decides it)
Raised by the user's simulated show `d3ef092dfafe` (2026-11-25), the lead agent's design; the user has not yet signed off the candidate prompt. Two fixes ship now. One candidate waits for Exp 27.

**The observation.** Show `d3ef092dfafe` ran on main before PR #78: the gen-v3 one-call generator, `bonsai-2:4b` on Ollama, the Haiku filter. (1) 2 of 6 bits fell back to the hand-written bank with `GenerationError: expected 3 threads of 5 lines`: bonsai returned the wrong shape. Exp 24 saw this 1 time in 60, but Exp 24 only ever wrote a show's first bit, with `premises_already_told` empty, and later bits send a growing list. A second show, on a server process started before #78 merged, hit `expected 3 threads of 5 lines..., got [1, 1, 1]` on all 5 jokes of bit 4 and played the bank each time. Shape `[1, 1, 1]` means bonsai packed each thread's jokes into one string. (2) 2 to 3 bridge lines played before nearly every bit: 7 to 18 s from a bit's end to its first joke (bonsai about 5 s, the Haiku filter about 3.6 s, then the voice). (3) The user reports that consecutive lines do not connect, like unrelated one-liners, in the bank bits (the bank is standalone one-liners by design) and in bonsai's own threads. (4) Show `e204ec80e62e` (gen-v4, on main before this PR) lost one thread on every joke of bit 3 with `expected a premise and 5 lines ..., got 1`: gen-v4's per-thread call also produces the packed shape, not only gen-v3; bit 0 of the same show lost one thread per joke to a transport ReadTimeout. Logged in dev-log.md.

**Fix 1: a counted reply schema.** The thread schema's `jokes` array now has `minItems: 5` and `maxItems: 5` (`generate.COUNTED_REPLY_SCHEMA`). Ollama's `json_schema` response format is meant to enforce it in the grammar while bonsai writes, which would stop a 1-item or 6-item array. **Judgment call, not measured:** nothing here checks that the installed Ollama honours `minItems` and `maxItems` for this model. The evidence that will settle it is Exp 26's and Exp 27's `lost_by_reason` (a `got N` count after this PR means the grammar did not hold) and `bad_replies`. **Anthropic does not take it.** Claude's structured output accepts `minItems` of 0 or 1 only and no `maxItems`; the SDK's own schema transform (`anthropic/lib/_parse/_transform.py`, checked offline in the repo's venv, SDK 2.14.0) keeps `minItems` 0 or 1 and moves anything else into the field's description text, where the model may or may not follow it. So `generate.schema_for(client)` sends the counted schema only on the OpenAI-compatible `json_schema` path (`llm.Client` with `json_schema=True`, which is how the Ollama writer is built) and the plain one everywhere else. The parser still enforces exactly 5 on both. Tests check both request bodies.

**Fix 2: re-ask once on a bad shape.** `ThreadGenerator` now passes `validate=parse` to `client.chat`, so `llm.run_with_retries` asks once more, inside the call's own deadline (the bit's shared 12 s), when a reply does not parse. A second bad reply drops only that thread, with its reason in `threads_lost`, as before. The re-ask is a second call, not a provider retry: `bad_replies` counts it and `retried` does not. It is not started when less than `llm.MIN_ATTEMPT_S` of the budget is left. **If the re-ask itself fails** (a timeout, a 503), `llm.run_with_retries` still returns the first bad reply, now as `llm.BadReply` carrying the failure, and `generate.parse_reply` records `re-ask failed: LLMError: ...` in `threads_lost`, so a timeout is not logged as a shape error. **Latency cost, stated:** a bit waits for its slowest call, so when one thread re-asks, the bit can hold for up to the full 12 s even though the other two finished in about 5 s; a re-ask only lands if the first reply came back fast. This is not measured: Exp 26 and Exp 27 report bit wall time p50 and p95 split by whether the bit re-asked (a bit counts as re-asked when its client's `bad_replies` rose), next to the loss and `bad_replies` counts. Because the call now re-asks, Exp 26's digest changes (the counted schema and the re-ask are part of it): it has not been run, so nothing is invalidated, and the stored-run check works as designed. Items 1 and 2 are fixes, not candidates: they ship whatever Exp 27 says.

**The candidate: gen-v5, a connected-bit prompt.** `generate.SYSTEM_CONNECTED`. Only the structure paragraph differs from gen-v4's; the mode note, the teens rule, the identity and the safety rules are shared text (one `_HEAD` and one `_TAIL`), and a test holds gen-v4's text byte for byte (its hash is pinned). gen-v4 says: opener, three tags, a closer that calls back to the opener. gen-v5 asks for one real bit: one premise; line 1 sets it up with the first laugh; each later line builds on the line right before it (a tag, then an escalation, a twist), and must not start a new topic or work as a standalone one-liner; the last line pays it off, with a callback to line 1 or the premise as the default. Same schema, temperature, K and timing. `generate.PROMPT_ID` selects the show's prompt and stays `gen-v4`; `ThreadGenerator(client, prompt_id="gen-v5")` is how the experiment uses the candidate. A test pins that the live default is unchanged.

| Option | For | Against | Choice |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) Keep the prompt; make continuity an SFT target | No generator change; fits the SFT plan | Waits for a trained model; the show runs gen-v4 until then | The fallback if Exp 27 fails |
| (b) A connected-bit prompt (gen-v5) | One line to flip; measurable now, free | A 4B model may ignore the structure; a judge has to say whether lines connect | **Candidate, tested by Exp 27** |
| (c) Write the bit line by line, each call told the lines before it | Connection is built in | 5 sequential calls at about 5 s each cannot fit 12 s; not DPO's one-request shape (D-110) | Rejected: judgment call, not measured |

**Exp 27 (the check).** `sim/thread_coherence.py`, notebook section "Exp 27 Connected threads". 20 seeded prompts (`c00` to `c19`; another seed than Exp 24's `p..` and Exp 26's `t..`), each with `premises_already_told` of 0 to 4 short, bland placeholder premises (the count cycles 0 to 4; the topics are a fixed list in the module, no joke text), so a prompt looks like a later bit of a show. Both arms (A: gen-v4, B: gen-v5) write each prompt through the show's own `generate.writer_client()` and the real `ThreadGenerator` (3 parallel threads), after `generate.warm_up`; the arm order alternates by prompt. **Judge:** Claude Haiku 5.5, thinking off, the safety filter's client settings, its own prompt and a `{"connected": bool}` reply, one call per thread. Blind to the arm; lines shown in plain punctuation (`sim/blind.py`). Question: one stand-up bit (one premise, each line builds on the one before, the order matters) or a list of separate jokes on a shared topic? Verdicts are cached by the thread text's hash in the run folder, so a rerun does not pay twice. The judge answers structure, not funniness. **Measurement:** per prompt, the connected rate over the delivered threads the judge answered, per arm.

**Bar, fixed before any run.** The mean over prompts of (B's rate minus A's), paired by prompt id, is above 0, and the paired bootstrap 95% CI (10,000 resamples of prompts, seed 27, the Exp 24 helpers) has its lower bound above 0. A prompt where either arm delivered no judged thread has no rate for that arm and drops out of the pairing; the number of paired prompts is reported. **The number that changes the decision:** that lower bound. **Reported beside it, no bar:** threads delivered and lost per arm by reason (bad shapes after the re-ask included), `bad_replies`, and bit wall time p50 and p95.

**Power of the bar, stated honestly.** 20 prompts with at most 3 threads each gives per-prompt rates of 0, 1/3, 2/3 or 1, and the bar needs a paired lower bound above zero, so it needs a large gain. A simulation (`thread_coherence.simulate_power`, 400 repetitions per cell; the inputs are made up, not measured: each prompt has its own connected rate drawn around A's 0.3 or 0.5, shared by both arms and shifted by the gain for B, 3 Bernoulli threads per arm) gives: false alarm about 4% at no gain; the bar passes about 27 to 30% of the time at a true gain of 0.1, about 70 to 72% at 0.2, and about 95 to 96% at 0.3. Reading: the experiment detects a gain of roughly 0.2 to 0.25 at 70 to 80% power and will often miss 0.1. A miss is "inconclusive for a small gain", not proof of none. More prompts, not more threads, is how to buy power: not done, a judgment call on the hours (about 5 minutes of Ollama time plus 120 judge calls).

**Spot-check (part of the decision, required whatever the bar says).** `python -m sim.thread_coherence review` shows the user 10 threads from a fixed seeded draw: 5 the judge called connected and 5 it called not connected, each half spread over both arms (3 and 2), the other half filling in when one is short, so it measures the judge's mistakes in both directions, not only its false "connected"s. The user sees the lines only (no verdict, no arm, no half) and answers y/n "is this one bit?"; the draw's makeup is recorded in the run folder and in the metrics. **Rule, fixed now:** a disagreement is a thread where the user's answer differs from the judge's verdict. If the user and the judge disagree on 40% or more of the threads to check (4 of 10), the judge is not trusted and Exp 27 is inconclusive whatever the CI says. Fewer than 10 threads to check (too few judged threads) is also inconclusive: a check that small cannot clear the judge (an earlier version voided on a count of 4 "no" answers, which could never fire with 3 or fewer connected threads, and let 3 "no" of 4 adopt). An unfinished check reads "pending review" and decides nothing, for a failing bar as well as a passing one.

**Power of the check.** If the judge is wrong on each thread independently with probability e (an assumption, not measured), the chance the check voids it is the chance of 4 or more disagreements in 10 (exact binomial, `thread_coherence.p_void`): 12% at e = 20%, 35% at 30%, 62% at 40%, 83% at 50%. These are the same figures as the old count rule, since the count was already 4 of 10. What changed is what is counted: both kinds of judge error now, over both arms. The check is weak: a judge wrong 30% of the time passes it 65% of the time, and 10 threads cannot tell 20% error from 30%. It guards against a badly broken judge, not a mildly noisy one.

**Decision rule.** Adopt gen-v5 in the show (a follow-up one-line PR sets `PROMPT_ID = "gen-v5"`) only if the bar passes and the spot-check is complete, has 10 threads and does not void the judge. If the bar fails with such a check, the show keeps gen-v4 and continuity becomes an SFT target (D-107's base model). A voided or too-small check is inconclusive: neither adopt nor conclude, and the judge or the sample is fixed first. The result belongs in a later entry, not an edit of this one.

**Cost and privacy.** About 120 judge calls (2 arms x 20 prompts x up to 3 threads), each about 300 input tokens and 20 output tokens at Haiku 5.5's prices: well under a cent, and a few cents even with retries. Thread text lives only in `data/thread_coherence/` (gitignored); the metrics file, tests and notebook hold numbers and reason classes only (a test checks the metrics file for text).

**Judgment calls, not measured.** That Ollama enforces the counted schema for this model (see Fix 1); that a Haiku judge's "connected" matches a person's (the spot-check measures it on 10 threads, a small sample, and only voids a badly broken judge); that a connected prompt does not make the jokes less funny (Exp 27 does not rate funniness: if gen-v5 wins, the 120-pair ranking tool is where that shows); that a re-ask lands inside 12 s often enough to matter; that the bank's standalone one-liners should stay as they are (they are hand-checked filler for a failed bit; fewer bank bits is the fix).

**Result, Exp 27 (2026-11-25, the user's run, `reports/metrics/thread_coherence.json`): `inconclusive_judge`. gen-v4 stays live; gen-v5 is not adopted.** The judge said B (gen-v5) beat A (gen-v4): mean paired difference +0.222, 95% CI +0.133 to +0.333 over 15 paired prompts, so the bar passed. The spot-check voided it. The user answered "is this one bit?" blind for the 10 drawn threads and disagreed with the judge on 6 of 10 (limit: under 4):

| | user: one bit | user: separate jokes |
|---|---|---|
| judge: connected | 2 | 3 |
| judge: not connected | 3 | 2 |

Agreement is 4 of 10, with a 95% Wilson interval of about 17% to 69%: it cannot be told apart from coin flips, and it is too low to trust, which is what the void rule asks. It erred in both directions equally (3 false "connected", 3 false "not connected"), which looks like noise rather than a bias a correction could undo, but 10 threads cannot rule a bias out. **15 of 20 prompts, stated:** 5 prompts dropped out of the pairing because one arm delivered no thread. Both arms lost 9 threads each, all `LLMError: transport: ReadTimeout`, in a cluster while the machine was slow (A: 2 bits with no thread, B: 3), so the loss hit both arms about equally and the bar's pairing is not biased toward one; it still leaves 15 prompts instead of 20. Delivered threads: 51 per arm, the judge called 34 of 51 connected for A and 45 of 51 for B. `bad_replies` 0 in both arms, writer 100% on the GPU after the run, 0 judge failures. Bit wall p50 5.92 s (A) and 5.24 s (B), p95 12.13 s and 12.03 s: slower than Exp 26's 3.53 s p50 because of the slow stretch, and not a clean timing read (Exp 26 is the timing check). **Not settled:** whether gen-v5 writes more connected bits; the next step is a judge the user agrees with (D-114), not a verdict on the prompt.

**Addendum 2026-11-26: judge v2 over the 20 prompts, and the extension to 100 (the user's call; no new D-number, no new design).** Written before any extension data exists. **Revised the same day after review:** the first version pooled all 100 prompts; the user then decided the 80 fresh prompts alone decide (below, and why).

*What is recorded now.* Exp 30 (D-114) kept judge v2 and trusted it, so Exp 27 was re-scored with v2 (`thread_coherence rescore --judge v2`). **Five bits were regenerated first.** c08-A, c09-B, c10-A, c13-B and c14-B had delivered no thread because of `LLMError: transport: ReadTimeout` in the slow stretch of the machine (the loss reason, not anything in their text, picked them: no judge had seen them). Their files were moved, not deleted, to `data/thread_coherence/timed-out-bits-2026-11-26/` and written again through the same path. Results now, from the three committed records:

| Judge | Prompts | Mean B - A | 95% CI | Bar (lower bound above 0) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| v1 (untrusted) | 15 (original record, commit 079b26b) | +0.222 | +0.133 to +0.333 | met, then voided by the spot-check (6 of 10 disagreements: `inconclusive_judge`) |
| v1 (untrusted) | 20 (after the rerun) | +0.200 | +0.117 to +0.283 | met, but v1 is not trusted (D-114); superseded by v2 |
| v2 | 15 (before the rerun) | +0.133 | -0.022 to +0.289 | failed |
| v2 | 20 (after the rerun) | +0.133 | 0.0 to +0.25 | **failed at exactly 0** |

Threads delivered and lost: A 57 and 3, B 60 and 0 (the 3 are all read timeouts, and all in one arm: c15-A lost one thread); bit wall p95 A 12.04 s, B 9.68 s; 0 bad replies. v2 called 32 of 57 A threads and 41 of 60 B threads connected. A lower bound of exactly 0 is not a pass (the bar says strictly above 0) and it is not evidence of no gain either: with 20 prompts and at most 3 threads each, the interval is coarse (the rates are thirds). **v1 is superseded by v2 (D-114):** its record now carries `superseded_by` and reads `pending_review` (the five new bits changed its spot-check draw); `review` refuses at every n and nobody relabels. The original 6-of-10 `inconclusive_judge` record is at commit 079b26b.

**The looks so far, stated plainly.** The 20 prompts have been looked at three ways before this extension: v1 over 15, v2 over 15, v2 over 20 after the five bits were rewritten (the rewrite came after the 15-prompt v2 result was seen, so the 20 is a different sample, not a re-test). The extension is the fourth look, and it was chosen *because* the 20 landed on 0.

*The extension.* Options and the user's choice:

| Option | For | Against | Choice |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) Call it: fail at exactly 0, keep gen-v4 | No more work | 20 prompts cannot tell +0.13 from 0 (power about 30% at a gain of 0.1); a bound at 0 settles nothing either way | Rejected |
| (b) Add prompts c20 to c99, pool all 100, judge v2 only | Simple | The pooled 100 contains the 20 that triggered the extension, which already average +0.133: simulated (made-up rates, 20,000 draws, 933 with a first-20 mean of 0.13 or more) the pooled bar then passes **17%** of the time with no true gain, against the nominal 2.5% | **Rejected after review** |
| (c) Add c20 to c99 and let the 80 fresh prompts alone decide; report the pooled 100 beside it | The deciding data are new, so the 20 cannot pull the answer; false alarm back near nominal | The 20 are not used for the decision (80 prompts, a little less power than 100) | **Chosen by the user (2026-11-26)** |
| (d) New user labels or a third judge | More trust in the judge | The user's labelling time; Exp 30 already trusted v2 | Rejected by the user |

**Pre-registered (fixed now, before any new data).**
- **Prompts.** n = 100, ids `c00` to `c99`. `prompts(100)[:20] == prompts(20)` (a test pins it, pins the 20's content hash and the 100's: `prompts_digest(100)` is `051b5b0239f6c5d7`); each prompt's context and `told` list come from its own seeded stream, so adding prompts cannot change the first 20. `told` keeps the round-robin 0 to 4 by prompt index. c00 to c19 and their stored bits and v2 verdicts are **reused, not regenerated**: `digest()` includes n, so the run folder and the records are keyed on `gen_digest(n)` = the 20-prompt digest (`1097f143`), and Exp 27's own record, folder and digests stay valid. That key cannot see c20 to c99, so the n=100 records' params also hold `prompts_digest_`, and every bit stores a `prompt_hash` that `gather` checks (a bit written for other prompts is refused; Exp 27's own 20 bits predate the hash and are accepted, pinned by the test above). `run --n 100` stops with an error if c00 to c19 are not in the folder. The 20-prompt records keep their file names; n=100 writes `thread_coherence_n100.json` and `thread_coherence_rescore_n100.json`. `--n` is 20 or 100.
- **Bar, same form, judge v2 only, over the 80 fresh prompts c20 to c99.** The paired mean B - A over those prompts (a prompt where an arm has no judged thread drops out; `n_paired` is reported), bootstrap 95% CI (10,000 resamples of prompts, seed 27) with the lower bound strictly above 0. **The number that changes the decision:** that lower bound. The pooled 100 is reported beside it (`pooled_bar`) and never decides. `rescore` stores the fresh-80 bar as `bar` (`bar_over: "fresh"`) and `adoptable()` accepts only that.
- **Adoption of gen-v5** (a later PR that sets `PROMPT_ID`) needs, besides that bar, D-114's conditions as `judge_calibration.adoptable()` now reads them: the user's B - A on Exp 30 not negative (it is +0.30, interval -0.20 to +0.79), v2's B - A on Exp 30's fresh 20 inside the user's interval (+0.20, inside), v2 kept and trusted, and the n=100 re-score current (its record holds n, `prompts_digest_` and the Exp 30 digest, verdict and agreement counts; another n or a changed Exp 30 makes it stale). If the bar passes and a condition fails, the user decides. If the bar fails, the show keeps gen-v4 and continuity becomes an SFT target (the fallback above).
- **Timeouts: mandatory and automatic.** Inside `run --n 100`, every fresh bit (c20 on) with no thread whose losses are all transport timeouts is written once more, in both arms alike, by loss reason only (no verdict exists yet). The first attempt is kept under `timed_out/`, a rewritten bit is flagged and never rewritten twice, and the n=100 record lists them in `bits_rewritten`. A bit that is still empty after its one rewrite stays empty and its prompt drops out of the pairing. No manual step remains.
- **No v1 and no review.** `run --n 100` writes bits only (no judge call at all); `review` refuses at every n and `score` refuses n=100 (at n=20 it prints that v1 is superseded). `rescore` stops without recording when any thread could not be judged (an outage), so the 100-prompt record never rests on a gap.

**Power and false alarm of the fresh-80 bar** (`thread_coherence.simulate_power(n=80)`, 1,000 repetitions per cell, 1,000 resamples, seed 27; the inputs are made up, not measured: each prompt has its own rate drawn around A's 0.3 or 0.5, shared by both arms and shifted by the gain for B, 3 threads per arm; the Monte Carlo error is about 1 to 1.5 points):

| True gain B - A | A's rate 0.3 | A's rate 0.5 |
|---|---|---|
| 0.00 (false alarm) | 3.8% | 1.8% |
| 0.10 | 73.1% | 66.6% |
| 0.13 (v2's 20-prompt estimate) | 90.9% | 88.2% |
| 0.20 | 100% | 99.8% |

(The pooled-100 table the first version quoted, 3% false alarm, was the unconditional figure; it did not hold given a first 20 that already looks like a win.) Reading: a pass on the fresh 80 means a gain of about 0.1 or more is likely and the false alarm is about 2 to 4%; a fail makes 0.13 or more unlikely (a miss is 9 to 12%) and 0.1 only unlikely (a miss is 27 to 33%), so a fail is "no gain worth a prompt change" rather than proof of zero. The pooled 100, reported beside it, is for reading only.

**Cost and runtime.** `run --n 100`: 80 new prompts x 2 arms at about 5.5 s per bit (Exp 27's p50 was 5.4 s, p95 12 s) is about 15 minutes of Ollama time, 20 to 25 if the machine has another slow stretch, plus the warm-up. `rescore --n 100`: about 470 new Haiku 5.5 calls (80 x 2 x about 2.9 threads; c00 to c19's 117 are cached), about 300 input tokens each (about 140,000 tokens): a few cents. No joke text is committed: the records hold numbers, ids and reason classes (a test checks).

**Judgment calls, not measured.** That c20 to c99 are as hard as c00 to c19 (same generator, same seeded context lists); that the 5 regenerated bits are an unbiased redraw (chosen by a failure that is independent of the verdict, but a redraw of 5 of 20 still changes the sample); that the made-up power inputs resemble the real thread-level correlation (the real per-prompt rates will show); **that a one-sided partial loss is harmless:** in the 20-prompt record A lost 3 threads and B none (c15-A delivered one thread fewer), so A's rates for those prompts rest on fewer threads, and a prompt with one judged thread has a rate of 0 or 1; the fresh 80 will show whether loss stays one-sided (delivered and lost per arm are reported).

**Result of the extension (2026-11-26, the user's run, `reports/metrics/thread_coherence_n100.json` and `thread_coherence_rescore_n100.json`): the fresh-80 bar passed, and gen-v5 is adopted.**

| Judge v2, B - A | Mean | 95% CI | Prompts | Role |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| The 80 fresh prompts (c20 to c99) | +0.206 | +0.117 to +0.298 | 80 | **decides: lower bound above 0, passed** |
| All 100 (with the first 20) | +0.192 | +0.115 to +0.268 | 100 | reported beside it, never decides |

Of the 80 fresh prompts, B beat A on 41, A beat B on 16, the rest tied. v2 called 123 of 239 A threads (51.5%) and 173 of 240 B threads (72.1%) connected on them. Writing, all 100 prompts: A delivered 296 threads and lost 4 (all read timeouts), B delivered 300 and lost 0; no bit was left with no thread, so `bits_rewritten` is empty; `bad_replies` A 5, B 0; bit wall time median/p95 A 5.22/8.45 s, B 4.59/6.90 s (gen-v5 is not slower; the first 20's older, slower stretch is in these numbers); the writer was 100% on the GPU after the run; 0 judge failures. The loss stayed one-sided (A 4, B 0), as the judgment call above feared, so I measured it: the only fresh prompt with a lost A thread is c93, and dropping it gives +0.203, CI +0.110 to +0.295 over 79 prompts, still above 0, so the loss does not carry the result. Also: a prompt with fewer judged A threads has a coarser A rate, but a difference this size (+0.21) is not what a few missing A threads would produce, and the fresh 80 that decide lost only 1 A thread and no B thread (A's other 3 losses are in the first 20).

**Adoption (D-114's conditions, `judge_calibration.adoptable()`: all five true).** The fresh-80 bar passes; v2 kept and trusted (agreement 15 of 20 against v1's 14); the user's own labels on Exp 30 favour B (one bit 7 of 10 against A's 4 of 10: +0.30, interval -0.20 to +0.79, not negative); v2's B - A on Exp 30 (+0.20) lies inside the user's interval; the n=100 re-score is current for the Exp 30 record. **Caveats kept:** the user's labels are 20 threads from one rater, v2 agrees with them 75% of the time and v2's wording counts a callback as a bit while gen-v5's prompt asks for one (D-114's guard checks the size, not the cause); the 20 first prompts were the fourth look, which is why only the 80 decide; funniness is not measured (the 120-pair ranking tool is where that shows).

**What shipped.** `generate.PROMPT_ID = "gen-v5"`. gen-v4 stays in `PROMPTS`, byte for byte (its hash `c0941a33c8dbdda8` is pinned, as is gen-v5's `d2cce7d55e8ff910`), and `ThreadGenerator(client, prompt_id="gen-v4")` still runs it. Nothing else about the generator changed (same schema, counted reply, re-ask, K, timing).

**Which stored records this makes stale, checked by running the digests.**
- *Unchanged (verified):* Exp 24 (`cg.gen_digest()` `2a512264` and `screen_local.prompt_digest()` `9cfd7e12`; both use the frozen `generate_v3`), Exp 27 (`1097f143`) and Exp 30 (`b774a07c`) (they name their prompts, not the live one), Exp 28 and Exp 29 (read session logs).
- *Stale, rerun optional:* **Exp 26** (`thread_timing`, digest `40d4f0e6`, now not matching; and Exp 23's digest too): it timed three parallel calls on gen-v4 and passed (p95 5.12 s). In the extension run gen-v5 lost no thread in 100 bits and its slowest tenth took 6.9 s (budget 12 s). That run is not Exp 26's test (no check that the 3 calls ran in parallel), so Exp 26 stays stale; judgment call, not measured, that it would still pass. The notebook shows a stale banner, then the stored table and checks, labelled as gen-v4's numbers. **Exp 23** (`evaluate_live`, one-call generator, already stale since D-110) stays so. A rerun of either is a judgment call, not needed for this decision.
- Shows logged from now on carry `gen-v5:` in their generator id; the stage-timing tool only skips ids starting with `bank`, so it counts them.


## <a id="d-112"></a>D-112 · 2026-11-25 — Bridge lines: skip lines the listener has already heard, fall back to filler (Exp 28 measures it) (user)
The user's call (2026-11-25), after the dev-log's "jokes feel heard before" row (show `e204ec80e62e`). The lead agent's design; the user chose the rule and confirmed the scope ("every show logged on this laptop").

**The observation.** In `e204ec80e62e` every cached bridge line was a replay from one earlier live show (`43251e36e02d`), and the jokes were all fresh (0 of 40 joke_played rows had been played in an earlier show). Exp 28 (`sim/evaluate_heard.py`, counts only) puts numbers on it: that show played 18 bridge rows, 17 cached and 1 filler, and **17 of 17 cached lines had been heard in an earlier show**. That figure is true by construction, not a finding: a cached line is the text a live show played, and that show is logged, so every cached line is heard the moment it exists. The dev-log row said 11 lines; the count from the logs is 18 (the 11 was a miscount or a different slice, so use this one). The two shows before it (`d3ef092dfafe`, `e9a42ccc6b36`) did the same: 17 of 17 and 5 of 5. Cause: `cache.popular_bits` picks the top 2 bits per mode by reward from live shows, deterministically, and one live show exists, so every show replays the same lines. The user is the only listener, so each replay is a repeat.

**What a replayed line already skips (checked in code, not new).** `show._cached_pool` runs every cached line through `cache.screen` (the rules layer, for this room's age; a teen room takes only bits from teen shows) before the show. A bridge or cached line writes only a `bridge` row (`show._play_cached`, `show._bridge`): never a `reaction` row, so it gets no label, no reward, no director update and no training example. `popular_bits` reads `joke_played` and `reaction` rows only. So repeats cost nothing in the data; the cost is only that the listener has heard them.

**The rule.** A line is **heard** when its normalised text (lowercase, spaces collapsed) is a `joke_played` or `bridge` row in any session log in `data/sessions`: any room, sim or live, any age. `cache.cached_for` builds the pool without heard lines (`popular_bits(sessions, heard=...)`). A bit whose lines were all heard gives its slot to the next-best bit of its mode (so a lower-ranked bit with unheard lines can fill `per_mode`); a bit with some heard keeps only the others. When no unheard cached line is left, the show falls back to the filler lines in `generate.BRIDGES` (`kind: filler`). `show._bridge` already keeps a per-show `used` list and says an unused filler line before repeating one (tested now); when all 10 are used in a long stall it repeats them in turn, which is accepted (the wait is at most `MAX_BRIDGES` lines and a repeat beats silence). The setting is `BRIDGE_SKIP_HEARD` (default on; `0`, `false`, `no` or `off` restores the old behaviour, for a new audience). The `cache` row is written on every llm-stack show, even with an empty pool, and carries `skip_heard` (the setting, so rule-off is distinguishable from a pre-D-112 log), `heard_dropped` (pool lines without the rule minus with it; counts only, omitted when the rule is off) and `bridge_seed` (the filler shuffle). `cache.build_pool` builds the pool and the count in one pass and reads the setting itself, so tests cover it; `main._show` calls it off the event loop.

**Consequence to read plainly.** The source show of a cached line is itself a logged session, so its text is always heard. With the user as the only listener the cached pool is therefore always empty and **cached bridges are effectively off for the user's own shows**; only filler runs. That is the intended outcome (the user agreed). The rule only does work if the heard set leaves out some shows, which is the later option below.

| Option | For | Against | Choice |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) Leave as is | Proven lines are the safe ones; nothing to build | The one listener hears the same 17 lines every show (Exp 28: 17 of 17 heard) | Rejected |
| (b) Rotate across shows (take a different top bit each show) | Some variety with a cache | One live show gives 17 lines: it runs out in a few shows, and the repeats come back; "rotation" needs state outside the logs | Rejected: judgment call, not measured |
| (c) Exclude heard lines, any show on this laptop | One rule, no new state (the logs are the memory); easy to switch off; tested by its guarantee | Cached bridges are off for the user's own shows; filler (10 lines) carries every wait | **Chosen** |
| (d) Per-listener id (heard = what this person heard) | The right model for a demo with strangers | Needs listener identity, which the app does not have; the user is the only listener now | Deferred (docs/explore-next.md #12) |

**Number that matters (Exp 28).** Exp 28 sizes the problem; it does not test the rule. `sim/evaluate_heard.py` reads the local logs (no model call) and records counts and session ids only (`reports/metrics/heard_bridges.json`). Its "100% of cached bridge lines heard" is true by construction (above), and "the pool the rule builds holds 0 heard lines" is the filter checked against itself; neither tells us anything the code does not already guarantee, and the real guarantee is tested on synthetic logs in `tests/test_cached_bits.py` (removing the filter fails them). The numbers that inform the decision are: **filler lines heard: 10 of 10**, and **pool size: 17 lines without the rule, 0 with it**. The count logged per show (`cache.heard_dropped`) is that difference (lines that would have been offered), not every heard line.

**Known limitation, flagged.** The 10 filler lines are fixed and repeat across shows. Exp 28 counts how many of the 10 the listener has heard: **all 10 of 10**. Four earlier shows played filler (the live show `43251e36e02d` alone played 12 filler lines, 2 of them a repeat inside the show, and one simulated show played 21), so the fallback this decision chooses is itself already heard. With cached bridges off, filler covers every wait (a show has about 7 waits of 1 to 3 lines), and `show._bridge` takes unused lines in the same order every show, so every show will play the same opening lines. Expect the same "I've heard these" complaint, now about filler. **Shuffle (user decision, in this PR).** The filler order is shuffled per show with a seed drawn at show start (`run_show(bridge_seed=...)`, `secrets.randbits(32)` when not given), and the seed is logged in the `cache` row so the show replays (invariant 6). Lines are rendered in the shuffled order, so the 10 still play with no repeat in a show while an unused one remains. It changes the order, not what is heard: all 10 are still repeats across shows, so it only stops the same line opening every wait. **Next (a follow-up PR, not this one):** 20 to 30 new filler lines the user approves by ear (each must clear `safety` and fit the 5 s sizing of `MAX_BRIDGES`), then per-listener heard sets (d). Not measured: how many lines it takes before the repeat stops being noticed.

**Judgment calls, not measured.** That "heard in any logged show" is the right scope for one listener (the user's call); that a normalised-text match is enough (a reworded line passes as new, and an exact repeat in other punctuation does not match); that 10 filler lines are tolerable for a few shows; that a repeat inside one long stall is acceptable.


## <a id="d-113"></a>D-113 · 2026-11-25 — Log where each bit's wait goes: a `prep` row per bit and the bridge overhang on its first joke (Exp 29 decides which stage to fix)
The lead agent's call, as instrumentation only; no behaviour of the show changes.

**The observation.** In show `6c09c721b0bb` the wait from a bit's start to its first joke was 9.8 to 17.2 s with 1 to 3 bridge lines per bit, while Exp 26 measured the writer at p50 3.5 s. The first joke always started exactly when the last bridge line ended, and bridges are never cut short. `show._prepare` runs generation, the safety prefetch and checks, the thread pick, then the first render (serial Kokoro, so it can queue behind bridge and cached renders). None of those durations was logged, so the log could not say which to fix.

| Option | For | Against | Choice |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) Fields on `bit_decision` / `joke_played` | No new event | `bit_decision` is written before prep runs, so its row would have to move or be rewritten | Rejected |
| (b) A new `prep` row per bit, written when `_prepare` returns, plus `bridge_overhang_s` on the bit's first `joke_played` | One row says the whole prep; older logs just lack it; replay ignores events it does not read | A new event type in schema v2 (additive: v2 logs without it still read) | **Chosen** |
| (c) Time stages offline from existing `at` stamps | No code change | Stage boundaries are not logged; a bridge's start only bounds prep from below | Rejected |

**The row.** `prep`: `bit`, `started_at`, `ready_at` (show clock, the timebase of `audio_start_at`), `generate_s` (all generator calls of the bit), `safety_s` (the prefetch and the per-candidate checks), `bank_s` and `bank_fallbacks` (bank consults inside prep; a bank consult after a failed render is not counted), `render_wait_s` (first line's render requested to ready), `render_queue_s` / `render_s` (the split of that wait, null when the voice cannot say), `other_s` (what no timer covers: the thread pick and building plans) and `first_render_failed`. `generate_s`, `safety_s`, `bank_s`, `other_s` and `render_wait_s` add up to `ready_at - started_at`, so a slow selector shows up as `other_s` and is not lost. `joke_played.bridge_overhang_s` is the bit's first played line's `audio_start_at` minus `ready_at`, at least 0. A pause that holds the line also counts in it; the logged pauses show when that happened.

**Queue versus synthesis.** `SerialTTS` holds one lock for the process; it now records, per calling thread, how long its last `synthesize` waited for the lock (`last_queue_s`). `show._render` reads it on the worker thread that ran the call. A voice without `last_queue_s` (fake voices, a future engine) logs the total only. The queue time does not include a wait for a thread in asyncio's default pool, which is small beside a Kokoro call.

**Number that matters (Exp 29).** `sim/stage_timing.py`. **The rule was revised after the Opus review of PR #81, before any data was recorded** (the first draft compared medians, which hide a stage that is large on a few bits and zero on most, and put no interval on its gate). Final rule, in the module docstring:
- Counted: bits 1 and up of shows from an LLM generator (the bank's stages are about zero). Bit 0 (cold model, the opener's renders, no bridge) is reported apart and does not decide. A bit is dropped when a pause overlaps its wait or its first render failed (counts reported).
- A stage's share is its total seconds over the total wait, so shares add to about 1 and a share is the mean seconds a fix could save per bit. Median and p95 stay in the table.
- Fix the largest-share stage when its share is at least 25% and `share - 1.1 x runner-up` has a 95% bootstrap interval above 0, resampling whole shows (2000 draws, seed 29; by show because bits in a show share a room and mood). Interval across 0: "inconclusive"; below 0: "no one stage". At least 2 counted shows and 16 counted bits, else "not enough data". With 2 or 3 shows the interval is wide, so "inconclusive" is the likely first answer.
- Candidate fixes: (a) end a bridge early or start the joke after the current line (bridge overhang), (b) render the first joke ahead of bridge and cached renders (render queue), (c) check each thread's safety as it arrives (safety). A dominant `generate_s`, `render_s`, `bank_s` or `other_s` has no fix among them. A voice that cannot split the render counts all of it as `render_s`, so the rule cannot drop it.
- Pause time inside a bit's wait would read as a stage, so those bits are dropped, not corrected.
- Run it on the LLM shows by name: `python -m sim.stage_timing <ids> --record`. With no ids it reads every log and still skips bank-generator shows.
Exp 29 waits for the user's next shows.

**Result (Exp 29, 2026-11-26): INCONCLUSIVE.** Recorded on every show on the pre-guess code (c474e51, #81) that logged `prep` rows: `0e102d77d795`, `c514a01f04f7`, `9fedcfa07c3f` (finished) and `4ee0e5658e25` (aborted after bit 2, 2 countable bits, no pause or failed render). The rule never excluded aborted shows, so it is applied as written: 23 counted bits over 4 shows, above the 16-bit and 2-show minimums. Shares of the total wait (p50 17.0 s, p95 26.7 s): generate 34%, render 28%, safety 22%, bridge overhang 17%, queue/bank/other about 0%. Interval of `share - 1.1 x runner-up` (generate against render): -0.13 to +0.12, across 0, so the rule says inconclusive: no single stage leads clearly. **No fix is built under either verdict.** On the 3 finished shows alone the writer led at 36% (interval +0.01 to +0.12, `no_candidate_fix`); the aborted show is voice-led (render 47% of its wait over 2 bits) and that flips the reading. **The first 3-show reading was a selection made after seeing the data (flagged in review), not the result.** Stage times depend on the writer, the filter and the voice, not on `audience.yaml`; all shows are the one sim room. D-115 has since removed most gaps, so this matters mainly for the gaps after a wrong guess. The writer's speed stays Exp 26's question. Record: `reports/metrics/stage_timing.json`.

**Judgment calls, not measured.** That one row per bit is the right grain (a bit has one prep); that 25%, 1.1x, the 16-bit and 2-show minimums and the share definition (total over total) are the right bars for "worth building"; that bit 0 is different enough to leave out of the decision; that dropping a paused bit beats correcting it; that the first played line is the right place for the overhang (a later joke never waits on the bit).

## <a id="d-114"></a>D-114 · 2026-11-25 — Thread judge: rewrite it from the spot-check, keep whichever of the two agrees with the user more (Exp 30 decides it) (user)
The user's call (2026-11-25), after Exp 27 came back `inconclusive_judge` (D-111). **Numbering:** D-114 and Exp 30 follow PR #81 (Exp 29, D-113).

**Pre-registered.** No Exp 30 data exists yet: everything below, including the extra gates added after review, is fixed before the fresh threads are written or labelled.

**Problem.** Exp 27's judge (v1, Claude Haiku 5.5) said gen-v5 is more connected than gen-v4 (+0.222, CI +0.133 to +0.333), but on the user's blind check of 10 threads it agreed with the user on 4 (2 yes/yes, 3 judge-yes/user-no, 3 judge-no/user-yes, 2 no/no): 40%, Wilson 95% interval about 17% to 69%, too low to trust. The question "is gen-v5 better?" cannot be answered with a judge the user does not agree with. v1's criterion ("one premise, each line builds on the one before, the order matters") is not what the user means by one bit.

**What the user's answers seem to follow (the lead agent's reading of the 10 threads, not measured).** One bit when a listener can follow one situation or one line of thought from the first line to the last: the same scene or people carry through, or each line follows from the one before (because, so, but, which means), or the end returns to the opening (a callback). Separate jokes when the lines jump between unrelated situations, even with a shared theme: a robot's battery, then overheating, then anxiety is a theme, not a bit. Structure only, never funniness. This is judge v2 (`thread_coherence.JUDGE_SYSTEM_V2`, short and plain, same `{"connected": bool}` reply).

**The 10 threads are v2's training set.** The criterion was drafted from them, so they cannot test it. A test of v2 needs threads neither judge was written from, which is the point of this experiment. This also means Exp 27's own spot-check can never be the judge check for a rescore.

| Option | For | Against | Choice |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) Keep v1; call Exp 27 unresolved | Nothing to build | The gen-v5 question stays open; the judge is already known to disagree with the user | Rejected |
| (b) Rewrite the judge (v2) from the spot-check, test old and new on fresh threads, keep the one that agrees more | Tests the fix on data it was not written from; keeps the cheap, repeatable judge | A rewrite fitted to 10 threads may not generalise (that is what the fresh sample tests); 20 labels are few | **Chosen by the user** |
| (c) Drop the LLM judge; the user labels every thread of Exp 27 | No judge to trust | 102 threads to label by hand; does not scale to the SFT/DPO loop that needs a judge | Rejected: judgment call, not measured |
| (d) Re-label Exp 27's threads (the user checks more of the same 102) and rescore v1 | Uses data already written | Does not fix the judge; v2's training threads are among them | Rejected |

**Fresh sample.** 10 new prompts (ids `f00` to `f09`, seed `exp30`; the already-told topics are a list that shares nothing with Exp 27's, and the prompts and the written texts are new), both arms (A gen-v4, B gen-v5) written through the show's own path (`thread_coherence.generate_bits`: warm-up, GPU check, resumable, the arm order alternating by prompt) into `data/thread_coherence/calib-<digest>/` (gitignored). Threads to label: 10 from arm A and 10 from arm B, a seeded draw that does not look at either judge's verdict (so neither judge picks its own test), one thread from each bit first and at most 2 from any bit, none identical to the 10 threads the user already reviewed (Exp 27's `review.json`). 20 and not 10 because agreement on 10 cannot separate two judges (below). Both judges judge the same drawn threads (about 40 Haiku calls). Exp 27's 10 reviewed hashes must load (the run fails loudly otherwise) and the number found is recorded as `reviewed_known`. The user labels blind: the lines only, shuffled, no verdict, no arm, and a question that does not borrow either judge's wording ("one bit, or separate jokes?"; Exp 27's prompt text was v1's own criterion and would have pulled the user toward it).

**Noise of the comparison, before any data.** Agreement is counted over the 20 labelled threads. If each judge were right on a thread independently with probabilities p1 (v1) and p2 (v2), the user's rule keeps v2 and the paired bootstrap CI (10,000 resamples of bits, seed 30) clears 0 this often (`jc.p_keep_v2`, exact; `jc.simulate_power`, 400 repetitions; these probabilities are made up, not measured):

| v1 right / v2 right | rule keeps v2 | CI lower bound above 0 |
|---|---|---|
| 0.5 / 0.5 (tie) | 44% | 2% |
| 0.4 / 0.4 (tie) | 44% | 2% |
| 0.5 / 0.7 | 88% | 26% |
| 0.4 / 0.7 | 97% | 47% |
| 0.4 / 0.8 | 99% | 73% |

Reading plainly: the rule is a point estimate on 20 labels. For two equal judges it keeps v2 about 4 times in 10 and v1 the rest (ties included), and the CI will usually include 0 unless v2 is much better (a 0.3 gap shows a clear CI about half the time). That is the user's chosen rule; the CI is reported so a 12-against-9 is not read as proof. Independence overstates the noise a little (two Haiku judges agree with each other on many threads, so fewer threads separate them) The CI resamples whole bits, so the 2 threads of one bit move together. Not buying more power: judgment call, not measured, on the user's labelling time (20 labels is about 10 minutes).

**Decision rule, fixed before any data** (also in the `sim/judge_calibration.py` docstring). Keep v2 if its agreement with the user's labels is strictly higher than v1's on the fresh 20 (the user's rule, a point estimate: it does not need the CI to clear 0); if equal, keep v1. The kept judge is trusted when its disagreement with the user on the 20 is under 40% (Exp 27's void rule: 8 or more of 20 disagreements voids it). Only a kept, trusted v2 may re-score Exp 27; this is enforced in `thread_coherence.rescore`, which refuses v1, an untrusted v2 or a missing record. v1 is never re-scored: its verdicts are Exp 27's own record, and if it is kept, Exp 27 stays `inconclusive_judge` and the gen-v5 question goes to the user's judge-free signal below or to SFT. Fewer than 20 threads to label gives `inconclusive_sample`; a missing label or a judge outage gives `pending`. Verdicts: `keep_v1_trusted`, `keep_v1_untrusted`, `keep_v2_trusted`, `keep_v2_untrusted`, `inconclusive_sample`, `pending`.

**The trust gate is noise-sized, stated plainly.** The kept judge must disagree with the user on under 40% of 20 (8 or more voids it). If its true per-thread agreement were 50%, 60%, 65%, 70% or 80%, it would pass this gate with probability 13%, 42%, 60%, 77% and 97% (exact binomial, `jc.p_trusted`). So the gate rejects a judge at chance, and a judge that is right only 6 times in 10 passes 4 times in 10. **Winner's curse:** the same 20 labels pick the winner (the higher of two) and certify it, so the kept judge's agreement is optimistic and the table above is kinder than reality. The record therefore reports a Wilson 95% interval for the kept judge's agreement, not only the point value.

**Extra gates on trust and on the downstream step (added before any data; the keep rule above is unchanged).**
- *Both classes.* To be trusted, the kept judge must be right at least once where the user says one bit and once where the user says separate jokes. A judge that always says yes can reach 13 of 20 against a user who says yes on 13, and would otherwise be kept and trusted. Each judge's yes-rate, Cohen's kappa and balanced accuracy are reported beside agreement, since agreement depends on the user's yes-rate.
- *Callbacks measure the prompt, not the writer.* v2 lets "the last line returns to the opening (a callback)" count on its own, and gen-v5's prompt asks for a callback. A rescore could then reward instruction-following. v2's wording is left exactly as written (changing it now would be tuning on the test); the guard is downstream. Each judge's confusion table and false-connected rate (of the user's "separate jokes", the share the judge called one bit) are split by arm, and the judge's B minus A one-bit share is reported next to the user's, with the user's interval resampled by prompt.
- *Re-score, and later adoption.* A stored re-score records the Exp 30 digest, verdict and agreement counts it was allowed under; if Exp 30 changes, the re-score is stale (the notebook flags it, `thread_coherence rescore` redoes it) and cannot support adoption.

**Adoption conditions for gen-v5 (the later PR; `jc.adoptable()` checks them from the stored records).** All of: (1) the rescored bar passes (mean B minus A above 0, CI lower bound above 0); (2) v2 was kept and trusted here (including both classes); (3) the user's own one-bit share on the fresh 20 is not lower for B than for A (B minus A of 0 or more); (4) the kept judge's B minus A on the fresh 20 lies inside the user's B minus A 95% interval (resampled by prompt, so the judge does not call B better than the user's own labels allow); (5) the re-score is current. The user decides any case on the edge; the PR states which conditions held.

**Beside the rule, no bar.** The share of threads the user called one bit, for arm A and arm B (with the CI of the difference): a signal that needs no judge, but only 10 labels per arm, so wide. Each judge's one-bit share per arm; the 2 by 2 confusion table per judge; threads where only v2 or only v1 matches the user.

**After a trusted v2: re-score Exp 27.** `python -m sim.thread_coherence rescore --judge v2` judges the stored Exp 27 threads with v2 (about 102 Haiku calls, cached under v2's own digest, `reports/metrics/thread_coherence_rescore.json`, no joke text) and recomputes the paired bar (mean B minus A above 0 and CI lower bound above 0) on the same prompts. The spot-check is not repeated: Exp 27's 10 answers are v2's training set, so they cannot clear it, and the fresh-20 agreement is the judge check instead. **Adoption of gen-v5 (a later, separate PR that sets `PROMPT_ID`)** needs both: the rescored bar passes, and v2 was kept and trusted here. The user's arm-A/arm-B one-bit shares are read next to that; if they point the other way, the PR says so and the user decides. If the bar fails with a trusted v2, the show keeps gen-v4 and continuity becomes an SFT target (D-111's fallback).

**Judge versions, and why Exp 27's record stays valid.** `JUDGE_SYSTEM` (v1) is byte-identical (its sha256 and `judge_digest` `00f9662b` and Exp 27's digest `1097f143` are pinned by tests). `JUDGES = {"v1": ..., "v2": ...}`; `judge_digest(version)`, `verdict_path`, `judge_thread` and `judge_all` take the version (default v1). v2 verdicts live in `judge_cache/<v2 digest>/`, so they cannot be read back as v1's (a test judges one thread under both and checks two calls and two files).

**Cost.** Writing 10 prompts on 2 arms takes about 3 minutes of Ollama time; about 40 judge calls here and about 102 on the rescore, each about 300 input tokens: a few cents together. The user's labelling is about 10 minutes. Thread text lives only under `data/thread_coherence/` (gitignored); the records hold counts and ids (a test checks both).

**Addendum 2026-11-26: Exp 30's result and the re-score (the user's call to extend is in D-111's addendum).** Exp 30 came back `keep_v2_trusted` on the 20 fresh labelled threads (`reports/metrics/judge_calibration.json`, counts only):

| | v1 | v2 |
|---|---|---|
| Agrees with the user | 14 of 20 (70%, Wilson 48% to 85%) | 15 of 20 (75%, Wilson 53% to 89%) |
| Disagreement (trust needs under 40%) | 30% | 25% |
| Cohen's kappa; yes-rate (user 55%) | 0.35; 85% | 0.48; 70% |
| Right where the user says one bit and where it says separate jokes | yes | yes (10 of 11, 5 of 9) |

v2 minus v1: +0.05, 95% CI -0.11 to +0.21 (v2 right alone 2 times, v1 alone once): the rule keeps v2 on a point estimate and the interval says the two judges cannot be told apart on 20 labels, which the noise table above predicted. The user called one bit in 4 of 10 arm-A threads and 7 of 10 arm-B threads (B - A +0.30, interval -0.20 to +0.79). v2's B - A is +0.20 (v1's +0.30), inside the user's interval. Both adoption guards held. **v2 is kept and trusted, and the Exp 27 re-score is allowed.**

**Re-score (2026-11-26).** Five timed-out Exp 27 bits were regenerated first (reasons, the stated risk and the files moved aside: D-111's addendum). v2 over 15 prompts: +0.133, CI -0.022 to +0.289, failed; over 20: +0.133, CI 0.0 to +0.25, **failed at exactly 0**, which is neither a pass nor a negative result. **Change to the re-score's bookkeeping:** its record now holds `n`, and it is current only for the same n and the same Exp 30 digest, verdict and agreement counts (`rescore_status(judge, n)`, `rescore_current(res, n)`); `adoptable()` reads the **100-prompt** record (`thread_coherence_rescore_n100.json`), not the 20-prompt one, because the user extended Exp 27 to 100 prompts with no new labels (D-111's addendum has the pre-registered bar, power and commands). **Revised after review:** the deciding statistic is v2's bar over the 80 fresh prompts c20 to c99 only, not the pooled 100 (the 20 triggered the extension, and a pooled test would pass about 17% of the time on no gain), so `adoptable()` accepts only a record with `bar_over: "fresh"`; the pooled 100 is reported beside it.. The 20-prompt re-score stays as a record and cannot support adoption on its own.

**Judgment calls, not measured.** That the user's labels are consistent (one rater, no repeats; adding blind repeats of Exp 27's 10 threads was skipped as not simple: they would need Exp 27's text and would change the 20); that the user saw v2's criterion before labelling the fresh threads, and Exp 27's 10 labels answered v1's wording while the fresh 20 answer a neutral question, so the two label sets are not the same instrument; that v2's reading of the user's 10 answers is what the user means (this experiment tests whether it agrees with the user on new threads, not whether the reading is true); that a Haiku judge can follow v2's criterion at all; that 20 labels from one rater are enough to pick between judges (the table above says when they are not); that 20 threads from 10 prompts per arm are enough for the paired CI, which now resamples whole bits (2 threads of one bit move together) and the by-arm difference by prompt.

**Adoption (2026-11-26).** The extension's fresh-80 bar passed (+0.206, CI +0.117 to +0.298) and `jc.adoptable()` returned all five conditions true (re-score current, bar passes, v2 kept and trusted, the user's B - A on Exp 30 not negative, v2's B - A inside the user's interval), so gen-v5 is adopted as the live prompt (D-111's addendum has the numbers, the caveats and the list of stored records the change makes stale). **Stated plainly:** the user's own labels (one bit 7 of 10 for B against 4 of 10 for A: +0.30, range -0.20 to +0.79) are too few to confirm the gain; the trusted judge's 80 fresh prompts carry the decision, and the judge agrees with the user 75% of the time.


## <a id="d-115"></a>D-115 · 2026-11-26 — Next bit: guess its mode after joke 3 and write it ahead, decide at the bit's end as before (Exp 31) (user)
The user chose the problem and approved the first option and the redirect after its result; the lead agent built and measured.

**The problem.** Waits of 12 to 17 s before a bit's first joke, filled with 2 to 3 filler lines per gap (shows `6c09c721b0bb`, `0e102d77d795`; "gaps between bits", "a lot of filler lines"). D-113's `prep` rows: generate about 3.8 s, safety 3.3 s, first render 3.0 s, plus up to 2.3 s of bridge overhang. Cause: `run_show` decides the next bit's mode only after the current bit's 5 jokes end, because the decision uses that bit's reward, so nothing of the next bit is prepared while jokes play (about 15 to 18 s of audio).

| Option | For | Against | Choice |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) Decide the mode after joke 3 (jokes 0 to 2 have reactions), write the bit during jokes 4 and 5 | Prep always hidden behind the audio; the user's first choice | The director is updated with a bit only at its end, so each pick is made one update behind, on a partial reward | Built and measured; **rejected** (Exp 31a: about 1.3 laughs per show lost, the cost is the delay) |
| (b) Guess the greedy mode after joke 3, write it, discard on a mismatch | The decision, random draws and rewards stay exactly as today, so there is nothing to lose in laughs; a miss costs only wasted calls | A wrong guess is wasted work and the gap stays on a miss; needs hygiene so a discarded bit leaves nothing behind | **Chosen** (Exp 31b: right 7 times in 10) |
| (c) Write the top 2 modes | Higher hit rate | Doubles the wasted calls and the serial voice's work; not measured | Rejected, judgment call, not measured |
| (d) Cap filler at 1 line per gap | No change to when prep starts | Leaves the wait; only changes what fills it | Rejected, judgment call, not measured |

**Design (b).** At joke index 2's reaction of bit N (`GUESS_AFTER_JOKE = 3`, not for the last bit), `app.predict.predict_mode` copies the director's LinUCB, teaches the copy the partial reward of bit N (`reward_v1` over the 3 reactions, only with at least 3 labels), and takes its greedy mode for bit N+1 with `last_mode` = bit N's mode and `last_reward` = the partial reward. It is a copy: the real matrices, the exploration RNG and the real selector are not touched. The bit is prepared on that mode while jokes 4 and 5 play.
