**Verdict rule.** `pass`: judge labelling of the training split may run next (a later PR), after the training rebuild above. `fail`: D-102's fallback, more of your votes or another judge (D-122 took "another judge"; Opus 5.5 also failed, 25 of 53, so DPO does not run on either judge's labels, see D-122's Result). DPO does not run on a judge that fails.

**Pre-merge review (2026-11-26, before any data).** Fixed: (1) a plain bit written after a tonight bit kept the tonight day's allow lists in the safety check, so names a plain show rejects could pass; a re-checked bit got stale lists. (2) A bit with no thread (Ollama down, every call timed out) was saved as finished and never retried, so an outage mid-run would silently empty the rest of the split. (3) The fixed cell order (teach tonight, test tonight, teach plain, test plain) and a progress line split by teach and test told you which questions were test pairs. (4) The disjointness key held the bit number for the tonight half, so the same request at bits 4 and 5 passed (planted; now caught). (5) Gists reused by two ranking prompts could land in both a teach and a test prompt (4 of 21 on a made-up 21-gist day); prompts sharing a gist now share a role. (6) A rebuild set aside every training bit, the unchanged plain half too (about 400 bits, an hour of writing); unchanged prompts now keep their bit, a second rebuild no longer collides, and the writer rewrites a record made for another prompt with the same id. (7) The record's key now includes a digest of every answer (test answers are not in the teach prompt). Recomputed and unchanged: the least a (21 of 30, 30 of 45, 38 of 60) and the power figures (1.4%, 74%, 91%, 98%, 99.9%; D-102's 66%, 90%, 98%). **Scope, not fixed:** the gate measures agreement on pairs you had a preference on; ties and skips are left out of teach and test alike, so the judge's accuracy on training pairs you would have tied is not measured. The tie and skip counts are reported for that.

**Self-agreement (the user's choice, 2026-11-26, set before any re-rank answer exists; reported only, no bar).** Why: no judge can agree with you more than you agree with yourself, so your own repeat rate is the ceiling for the judge's number. How: after the 120 pairs are decided, `rerank` asks 20 of them again (seeded: 5 in each of teach and test for each of tonight and plain, so 10 teach and 10 test), the same two threads with the sides re-drawn by a new seed, no skip, and never shows the earlier answer. Answers go to `data/dpo/rerank.json` after each one (resumable); `rank.json` is never written. `score` reports how many of the 20 got the same pick (a tie on the re-rank counts as a disagreement and is shown separately), with a Wilson 95% range, and beside it the judge's agreement on the test pairs among the 20 (teach pairs were shown to the judge, so they are left out there). With no `rerank.json`, `score` says "not run". The record's key includes a digest of the re-rank answers, so it reflects them; the Exp 36 verdict never reads them (tested).

**Result (2026-11-27): the bar was not met; verdict `fail`.** Run by the user on this branch (`judge`, then `score --record`; the re-rank was skipped by the user's choice, so self-agreement is not run). Record: `reports/metrics/dpo_judge_gate.json` (numbers only).

| Check | Result | Bar |
|---|---|---|
| Test pairs answered the same way in both orders (c) | 46 of 60 | at least 30: met |
| Of those, the judge picks the user's thread (a) | 24 of 46 = 52%, Wilson 95% range 38% to 66% | lower end above 50%: **not met** (30 of 46 was the least) |
| Pairs where the orders disagree | 23% (14 of 60) | beside the bar |
| Tonight half: consistent, agreeing | 20 of 30, 11 | beside the bar |
| Plain half: consistent, agreeing | 26 of 30, 13 | beside the bar |
| Strict count (a of all 60) | 24 of 60 (Wilson 95% 29% to 53%) | beside the bar |
| Half-credit rate (a flipped pair earns half) | 0.517 | beside the bar |
| Ties / skips while ranking | 12 / 12 of 144 answers | beside the bar |
| Self-agreement (re-rank of 20) | not run (the user skipped it) | reported only |

Reading. The judge is consistent often enough (76% of pairs) but its consistent answers are a coin flip against the user's taste: 52% with a range that includes 38% to 66%. The half-credit rate (0.517) and the strict count agree. Neither half is better: 55% on tonight, 50% on plain, on 20 and 26 pairs. The test could not tell a judge that is 50% from one that is 60% right per pair, but it was built to detect about 65% and up (power table above, 74% at 65%), and the point estimate is 52%. **Haiku 5.5 taught with 60 same-model pairs does not predict the user's taste, so DPO does not run on its labels** (D-102's rule). The Haiku result stays recorded as a fail whatever any later judge does. Whether the user's own repeat rate is also near 50% is unknown, because the self-agreement re-rank was skipped: if it is, no judge could pass, which would be a finding about the pairs, not about Haiku. D-122 records the next step.

**Ranking time (user's report, not measured).** The user reports the ranking took about 12 to 16 hours over Nov 26 to 27, not the planned 70 minutes (35 s a pair was a guess). The answers carry no timestamps, so this was not measured. From now on every new rank and rerank answer carries `at` (UTC, ISO, seconds); old answers stay as they are, and the record's key still covers them unchanged (tested). The planning figure for any future ranking should be counted in hours, not minutes: it matters for the "collect fresh pairs" option in D-122.

**Update (2026-11-27, re-rank done).** The user re-ranked 20 of the 120 pairs (10 teach, 10 test; same two bits, sides re-drawn, earlier answer hidden). Same pick again on 12 of 20 (60%, likely range 39% to 78%, 95%); 3 re-picks were ties and count as disagreements. Ignoring ties, 12 of 17 decided re-picks (71%, 47% to 87%). Simple model, a judgment call: the user picks their true favourite with probability p each time, so repeat agreement is p^2 + (1-p)^2. 60% gives p of about 72% and 71% gives about 82%. So even a perfect copy of the user's taste would agree with any single answer only about 72% to 82% of the time: that is the ceiling for a judge on these pairs. The judges (52% and 47%) sit below it, but with 20 re-ranked pairs the ranges overlap, so the data cannot fully separate "the judges do not share the user's taste" from "the pairs are too close to call". Both stay open. On the 10 test pairs among the 20: Haiku agreed in both orders on 3 (4 had orders disagreeing), Opus on 4 (2 disagreeing). Both verdicts are unchanged (fail). Timing, from the `at` fields: median 62 s between consecutive answers (19 gaps), first to last answer 31 minutes; the first answer's own time is unknown.

## <a id="d-122"></a>D-122 · 2026-11-27 — Try Claude Opus 5.5 as the judge on the same 120 pairs, with a stricter bar for the second look (Exp 37) (user)
Decided by the user, 2026-11-27, after Exp 36 failed (D-121). The model, the bar and the correction below are fixed **before any Opus answer exists**; nothing from Exp 37 can change them.

**Choice.** Run the gate again with one other model, on the **same 60 teach and 60 test pairs**, rather than collect fresh pairs. The model is **Claude Opus 5.5 (`claude-opus-5-5`)**, one model only. Same judge prompt, same 60 teach examples in the same order, same both-orders protocol, same reply schema and `effort: low` as the Haiku run; only the model id changes. Differences forced by the model, all of them infrastructure: (a) Opus 5.5 returns a 400 if thinking is disabled at any effort, so thinking is on (adaptive, effort low), where Haiku's ran with it off; the client omits the `thinking` field for this model. (b) Adaptive thinking is slower than the safety check's 12 s / 25 s timing, so Opus gets 90 s per call and 240 s per judgment (a timeout would otherwise count as a missing answer, an `incomplete` verdict). (c) The teach examples (about 18,000 tokens, identical in all 120 calls) are marked for prompt caching (`cache_system`, off by default, so no other caller changes). Neither (a) nor (c) changes what the model is asked; (a) means "same settings" is true for the prompt and effort, not for thinking, which Haiku had off. Separate cache `data/dpo/judge/<digest>/` (the model is part of the digest) and a separate record `reports/metrics/dpo_judge_gate_opus.json`. Haiku stays the default for every command, so Exp 36 reproduces (checked: the Haiku digest `4af1cf27` and its score are unchanged).

**Why a stricter bar for a second look at the same test set (set now, before any Opus answer).** The 60 test pairs were already used to judge Haiku. Trying a second judge on them and stopping when one passes is optional stopping: with two chances to cross the same line, the chance that a judge with no real skill passes roughly doubles. Correction (**Bonferroni for two looks**): the Wilson lower end must be above 50% at the **97.5%** level, z = 2.2414 (the 95% bar spends 5% on one look; two looks at 2.5% each keep the total near 5%). **Pass iff c >= 30 AND the lower end of the 97.5% Wilson range of a/c is above 50%.** This is a **judgment call on the correction method, not measured**: a plain Bonferroni is the simplest and mildly conservative (the two looks are not independent, since the same pairs and examples are used), and a stricter or looser choice is defensible. The Haiku result stays a fail under its own 95% bar; it is not re-scored under this one (it fails there too: 24 of 46 is far below 31).

| c (consistent pairs) | least a, Exp 36 bar (95%) | **least a, Exp 37 bar (97.5%)** |
|---|---|---|
| 30 (the floor) | 21 | **22** |
| 46 (Haiku's c) | 30 | **31** |
| 60 (all) | 38 | **39** |

Power (computed, exact binomial, same assumptions as D-121: the judge is right with probability p in each order, the two orders' errors independent, no position preference, 60 test pairs). Chance of passing:

| Judge truly right per order | Exp 36 bar | **Exp 37 bar** |
|---|---|---|
| 50% | 1.4% | **0.7%** |
| 65% | 73.8% | **69.5%** |
| 70% | 91.2% | **90.7%** |
| 75% | 98.2% | **98.2%** |
| 80% | 99.9% | **99.9%** |

The correction costs about 4 points of power at 65% and nothing from 75%, and halves the false-pass chance at 50%. Both tables are pinned by tests.

**Options and trade-offs.**
| Option | For | Against | Chosen |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) Claude Opus 5.5 on the same pairs | Strongest model we can call; zero new ranking time; same prompt and protocol | A second look at used test pairs, so a stricter bar; costs more per call; thinking cannot be turned off | **Yes (the user's choice)** |
| (b) Claude Sonnet 5.5 on the same pairs | About half Opus's price (2 and 10 dollars per million tokens against 4 and 20) | Weaker; the same second-look problem; if it fails the user pays for another try | No: a judge that fails again leaves no time, so the strongest model first |
| (c) Fresh pairs for a new teach and test set | Clean test: no second-look correction, and the full 95% bar | The ranking took 12 to 16 hours for 120 pairs (user's report); with the deadline (Dec 8 target, Dec 14 report) another round costs most of a day or two the user may not have | No (the user's choice): reuse the pairs |
| One model only | Models are not shopped until one passes; a third look would need a second correction | If Opus fails there is no fallback judge | **Yes, one model only**, chosen before running |

**Cost estimate (before running; no API call made).** Local count of one request from the real prompts under `data/dpo` (read only): system prompt (teach examples) 72,474 characters, one user message about 1,207 characters on average. At about 4 characters a token: **about 18,100 tokens of system plus 300 of pair, 18,400 in a call**; 120 calls. The count is a characters/4 approximation, it may undercount by up to about 20%. Output: the reply is a few tokens of JSON, but thinking tokens count as output; assumed 200 to 1,000 per call at effort low (unmeasured: the first run prints the real totals). Prices (Opus 5.5, per million tokens, from the Claude API model table cached 2026-11-03: 4 dollars in, 20 out, cache reads 0.20; a cache write is assumed at the standard 1.25 times the input price, 5 dollars; check the current price list before relying on it):
| | Input | Output (200 to 1,000 per call) | **Total** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Without caching | 120 x 18,400 = 2.21M tokens = **$8.83** | $0.48 to $2.40 | **about $9.3 to $11.2** |
| With caching | 1 write 18,100 x $5 = $0.09, 119 reads 2.15M x $0.20 = $0.43, pair text 36k x $4 = $0.14: **about $0.66** | $0.48 to $2.40 | **about $1.1 to $3.1** |
Caching needs the 120 calls close together (the cache lives 5 minutes after each read); `judge` runs them back to back, and a resumed run pays the write again. A `judge` run prints the real token totals, including cache reads and writes.

**If Opus passes.** The training pool's labelling may run with Opus (a later PR), after the training split is rebuilt with 3 or more days. Labelling cost for 800 prompts x 3 pairs x 2 orders = **4,800 calls**: with caching (same teach prefix) about **$23 input** (4,800 x (18,100 x $0.20 + 300 x $4) per million) plus **$19 to $96 output** = **about $42 to $119**; without caching about **$353 input + $19 to $96 = $372 to $449**. The Batch API (half price, not planned) could cut it further. **If Opus fails:** DPO does not run; the project ships without DPO, and the two negative results (Haiku 24 of 46, Opus's own numbers) go in the report as the answer to "can a frozen LLM judge stand in for your taste on same-model pairs". Fresh pairs stay possible only if the user picks them with the deadline in view.

**What is not controlled.** The same 60 test pairs are used twice (corrected above, not eliminated). Thinking is on for Opus and off for Haiku, so a difference between the two results is the model and the thinking together. The user's own repeat rate (the re-rank) was not collected, so a failure cannot be separated from "the pairs are too close for anyone to agree on".

**Judgment calls, not measured.** The Bonferroni correction for two looks and z = 2.2414; Opus's timeouts (90 s, 240 s); 200 to 1,000 output tokens a call; the 1.25 cache-write price.

**Commands (the user runs these; the PR runs none).**
```
python -m sim.dpo_pairs judge --model opus --env-file backend/.env   # 120 Opus calls, cached in data/dpo/judge/<opus digest>/; prints token totals
python -m sim.dpo_pairs score --model opus                          # table on screen, no record
python -m sim.dpo_pairs score --model opus --record                 # writes reports/metrics/dpo_judge_gate_opus.json (Exp 37)
python scripts/run_notebook.py notebooks/decisions-model.ipynb      # Exp 37 section fills in
```
**Result (2026-11-27): the bar was not met; verdict `fail`.** Run by the user on this branch (`judge --model opus`, then `score --model opus --record`; the re-rank was not run, so self-agreement is not run). Record: `reports/metrics/dpo_judge_gate_opus.json` (numbers only). Per D-122's rule, **DPO does not run on either judge's labels.** The user confirms the next step separately.

| Check | Result | Bar |
|---|---|---|
| Test pairs answered the same way in both orders (c) | 53 of 60 | at least 30: met |
| Of those, Opus picks the user's thread (a) | 25 of 53 = 47%, Wilson 97.5% range 33% to 62% | lower end above 50%: **not met** (35 of 53 was the least) |
| Pairs where the orders disagree | 12% (7 of 60) | beside the bar |
| Tonight half: consistent, agreeing | 29 of 30, 13 | beside the bar |
| Plain half: consistent, agreeing | 24 of 30, 12 | beside the bar |
| Strict count (a of all 60) | 25 of 60 (Wilson 97.5% 29% to 56%) | beside the bar |
| Half-credit rate | 0.475 | beside the bar |
| Ties / skips while ranking | 12 / 12 of 144 answers | beside the bar |
| Self-agreement | not run | reported only |

**Cost (measured, from the run's printed totals).** 120 calls: input 47,808 tokens, output 1,440 (thinking included, about 12 a call), cache read 2,864,806, cache write 24,074. At the prices above (4 in, 20 out, 0.20 cache read, 5 cache write, per million): 47,808 x 4 = $0.191, 1,440 x 20 = $0.029, 2,864,806 x 0.20 = $0.573, 24,074 x 5 = $0.120. **Total $0.91**, below the $1.1 to $3.1 estimate because Opus at effort low used almost no thinking tokens (1,440 against the assumed 24,000 to 120,000). Prices are the cached 2026-11-03 table, not re-checked.

**Diagnostic: do the two judges agree with each other? (reported only, no bar; `dp.judge_vs_judge`, read only on the two stored caches, no calls).** On the test pairs both judges answered the same way in both orders: **41 pairs**. Likely ranges are Wilson 97.5% throughout.

| On those 41 pairs | Count | Share | Likely range |
|---|---|---|---|
| haiku-5.5 judge and opus-5.5 judge pick the same thread | 32 | 78% | 61% to 89% |
| haiku-5.5 judge picks the user's thread | 20 | 49% | 32% to 65% |
| opus-5.5 judge picks the user's thread | 19 | 46% | 30% to 63% |

Teach pairs: neither cache holds an answer for them (0 found), as expected: the 60 teach pairs are in the judge's prompt, not asked.

Reading, cautiously. The two judges agree with each other far more (78%, range 61% to 89%) than either agrees with the user (46% to 49%, ranges that include 50%). That fits a shared taste between the two models that differs from the user's on these pairs. It also fits the user's picks on close pairs being noisy. Self-agreement was not run, so the two cannot be separated here. Earlier evidence points the same way: 24 of 144 ranking answers (17%) were ties or skips, so some pairs were hard for the user too. The judge pair share is on 41 pairs picked after seeing both results, so it is descriptive, not a test.

**What it means for the report.** Two strong judges (Haiku 5.5, Opus 5.5, the second with thinking on and a stricter bar) sit at about a coin flip against one person's taste on close pairs from one writer: 24 of 46 and 25 of 53. That is a clean negative result for "an LLM judge as a stand-in for one person's taste on close pairs", not a result about Haiku or Opus in particular. What the data cannot say: whether any judge could do better (no user repeat rate), whether a judge does better on pairs less alike, or whether more teach pairs help (60 were used). The ship decision, per D-122's stated fallback, is the user's; this entry only records that DPO does not run on either judge's labels.

**Update (2026-11-27, after both records; pre-merge review).** (1) The re-rank is running: the user started `rerank` after Exp 37 was recorded (9 of 20 answered at the review). "Skipped" and "not run" in D-121's and D-122's Results describe the state when the two gates were recorded. Both verdicts are fixed and never read the re-rank (tested); when it finishes, `score --record` and `score --model opus --record` add self-agreement beside them, and the Opus record also stores the judge-against-judge numbers (`judge_vs_judge`), so the notebooks read them from `reports/metrics` instead of the gitignored caches under `data/dpo/judge/`. Pending, not yet a result. (2) The cost estimate's token count was low: the measured cached prefix is 24,074 tokens (119 reads of 24,074 = the 2,864,806 read), against the 18,100 estimated from characters / 4, an undercount of 33%, beyond the 20% allowed for. The run still cost $0.91 because output was tiny. Any later Opus labelling estimate should use about 24,100 tokens a call: input about $31 for 4,800 calls with caching, not $23. (3) The next step is the user's (2026-11-27): D-123, DPO toward the AI judge's taste with a second writer on the user's pairs, on qwen3:4b-instruct, in a stacked PR.

**Update (2026-11-27, re-rank done).** The re-rank is finished and both records now carry self-agreement: 12 of 20 same pick (60%, 39% to 78%; 12 of 17 ignoring ties, 71%, 47% to 87%), p of about 72% to 82% under the simple model, so a judge's ceiling is about 72% to 82%; Haiku and Opus at 52% and 47% are below it but the ranges overlap, so "no shared taste" and "pairs too close to call" are not separated (detail in D-121's Update). The Opus record also carries `judge_vs_judge` (32 of 41). Both verdicts are unchanged. For D-123's Exp 38: its eval bar must account for this noise; a separate PR will set it.
## <a id="d-123"></a>D-123 · 2026-11-27 — DPO toward the AI judge's taste (RLAIF), plus a writer trained on your own pairs, judged by your blind votes (Exp 38) (user)
Decided by the user, 2026-11-27, after Exp 36 and Exp 37 both failed the judge gate (D-121, D-122). **This overrides D-122's rule "DPO does not run on either judge's labels".** The user's reason: SFT and DPO are the course's main deliverable, so they are trained anyway and the report says honestly what they are. Everything marked "judgment call, not measured" below is exactly that.

**What the project may and may not claim.** Haiku picked the user's bit on 24 of 46 consistent pairs (52%, range 38% to 66%, Exp 36) and Opus on 25 of 53 (47%, range 33% to 62%, Exp 37); they agree with each other on 32 of 41. So writer B is trained **toward an AI judge's taste** (RLAIF), and the write-up must say "DPO toward an AI judge's taste", never "toward the audience's taste" or "toward the user's taste". Only writer C is trained on a person's choices (the user's), and only 120 of them.

**The three writers** (A and B and C all start from `qwen3:4b-instruct`, D-124):

| Writer | What it is | Trained on | Served as |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A** | The base, no training | Nothing | `qwen3:4b-instruct` (stock) |
| **B** | SFT, then DPO on the SFT model | SFT: for each training bit, the thread Haiku preferred most. DPO: every pair Haiku labelled consistently (both orders) among the first three clean threads of a bit | `humor-qwen3-B` |
| **C** | DPO on the base | The user's 120 decided pairs (the 60 teach and 60 test pairs of Exp 36, from bonsai's ranking split) | `humor-qwen3-C` |

**The training pool is rewritten by the new base (on-policy).** `python -m sim.dpo_pairs write --split train --writer qwen3:4b-instruct` writes the same 800 training prompts (the split as built, frozen) with qwen3, into `data/dpo/bits/train-qwen3/`; `topup` works the same way. Each bit stores the writer's name and each thread the raw reply text. Why: DPO's pairs should be samples from the model being trained; bonsai's threads are not. D-121 said the training split should be rebuilt with three or more tonight days before labelling. **It was not rebuilt:** only one day (2026-11-26) is stored, and a rebuild needs more days fetched first, which the schedule does not allow. So the tonight half's 400 prompts reuse one day's approved headlines (21 gists, round-robin, on the 100 headline bits), and the held-out eval prompts do too: they are held out by prompt, not by topic. A model that learned those headlines would look better on the eval's tonight half than on a new day. **Reported as a limit, not controlled.**

**Writer C is off-policy for the new base, and it says so.** The 120 pairs are bonsai-2:4b's threads. DPO assumes the pairs come from (or near) the model being trained; the qwen3 base writes differently. The bits had no stored reply (that came with this PR), so C's chosen and rejected replies are the parsed thread re-serialised as the schema shapes it (`json.dumps` of premise and jokes), not bonsai's exact bytes: a judgment call, not measured. The prompts are the real ones (rebuilt from each bit's stored request, split back into its system and user messages).

**Labelling (the Exp 36 configuration, nothing else).** `python -m sim.dpo_pairs label --split train --writer qwen3:4b-instruct --env-file backend/.env`: Claude Haiku 5.5, thinking off, taught by the same 60 teach pairs, the same prompt and schema. All pairs among the first three clean threads of a bit (up to 3 a bit), each in both orders; a pair whose two orders name different threads gets no label and is dropped (D-121's rule). The 18,000-token teach prefix is a cache breakpoint. Answers are cached one file each under `data/dpo/labels/cache/<digest of the taught prompt>/` (resumable, a changed teach set starts a new cache), the labels land in `data/dpo/labels/train-qwen3.jsonl`, and the existing rebuild guard on `data/dpo/labels/` freezes the training split once labelling starts (a rebuild is also refused once any other writer's training bits exist). Four calls run at once after the first, which runs alone so it writes the cache. The run prints the cost so far and keeps a running total across restarts. **The Message Batches API (half price) is not used:** the repo's Claude client has no batch call, and building and testing one without being allowed to call the API was not worth the risk; the cost is about a dollar anyway.
**Cost estimate (no call made).** 800 bits, at most 3 pairs, 2 orders: **at most 4,800 calls**; fewer, because some bits have under 2 clean threads. Prices are Haiku 5.5's from the repo (`PRICE_PER_MTOK`: $0.10 in, $0.50 out per million tokens), with a cache read at 0.1 times and a cache write at 1.25 times the input price (the standard multipliers, assumed, as D-122 assumed them for Opus). One call is about 18,100 cached prefix tokens plus 300 of pair, output about 12 tokens (characters / 4: may undercount by 20%). With caching: 4,800 x 18,100 x $0.01 per million = $0.87, 4,800 x 300 x $0.10 = $0.14, output $0.03: **about $1 (up to $1.3 if the count is 20% low)**. Without caching: 4,800 x 18,400 x $0.10 = **$8.8**. Pairs expected to survive: Exp 36 dropped 14 of 60 pairs for disagreeing orders (23%), so about 1,850 consistent pairs of the 2,400 at most, **an unmeasured guess**: the DPO set for B lands between roughly 1,200 and 1,850 pairs.

**Training (Explorer; the user runs every job).** LoRA on all linear layers (r 16, alpha 32, dropout 0.05), fp16 mixed precision with the LoRA weights in fp32 (the V100 has no bfloat16), gradient checkpointing, SDPA attention, one pair per device with gradient accumulation 8. DPO: TRL `DPOTrainer`, `ref_model=None` (the reference is the same network with the adapter off), `precompute_ref_log_probs=True`, beta 0.1, learning rate 1e-5 (the brief said about 5e-6 to 1e-5; 1e-5 is the top of it, because LoRA moves slowly), one epoch for B, three for C (120 pairs are about 45 optimizer steps otherwise too few); SFT: learning rate 1e-4, two epochs, loss on the reply only. Checkpoints every 25 steps, a resubmit resumes, a callback saves and stops at 7.5 h (the job limit is 8 h), and a NaN loss or gradient norm stops the run with exit code 3. **All of these numbers are judgment calls, not measured**; the 10-step smoke test (`explorer/smoke.sbatch`) prints seconds per step and peak GPU memory, and the user should read it before submitting anything long. If the DPO reward accuracy stays at 0.5 after a few dozen steps the learning rate is too low (raise `LR=`); if the loss goes to NaN, lower it.
**Prompt text.** Training uses Ollama's rendering of the chat template, not Hugging Face's, because the pool and the show both go through Ollama and the trained model will be served through it (D-124). They differ by one blank line after the system text (`system\nSYS\n\n<|im_end|>` against `SYS<|im_end|>`); the difference was found by rendering the real template blob with Go's template engine, and tests pin it (`tests/test_chatml.py`). The smoke job checks the real tokenizer against the Hugging Face form.
**Reward hacking (writer B).** DPO on a judge's labels optimises for what the judge likes, including its quirks (length, a favourite structure, safe-sounding wordplay). Haiku and Opus agree with each other (78%) more than with the user, so they share a taste that is not the user's; B can get better at that taste and worse at the user's. **Haiku cannot evaluate B**: it wrote B's training labels, so its reading of B is circular. That is why the deciding measure is the user's own blind votes.
**Time and cost on the cluster (estimates, not measured).** SFT about 800 rows, two epochs: on the order of an hour; DPO B on 1,200 to 1,850 pairs: roughly 3 to 6 hours; DPO C: under an hour; merge plus GGUF about 30 minutes each, on the CPU partition. The smoke test measures the real seconds per step; multiply by the step counts. `courses-gpu` runs one job at a time, so the three trainings queue.

**Exp 38, the deciding measure (set before any data exists).** Held-out eval prompts: 60 new seeded prompts of the pool's distribution (half with tonight's context), none equal to a ranking or training prompt or to Exp 24, 26, 27 or 30 (checked at export). Each served writer writes one bit per eval prompt through the live request path (`eval write`; the safety check runs on its lines, bit time is the writer's calls only, as in Exp 26). The first clean thread of a bit is the one compared. **Votes:** 30 blind pairs, **15 B against A and 15 C against A**, on different prompts, the trained writer on a seeded side, 1 / 2 / tie / quit, no skip, every answer stamped with the time (the ranking took far longer than planned and nothing recorded how much). The user's earlier choice was 30 shared pairs; three writers cannot all be compared well in 30, so **B against C is judged only by Opus**. **Opus 5.5 judges** all three comparisons over all 60 prompts in both orders (360 calls; D-122 measured $0.91 for 120 calls, so about $2.7), with the same 60 teach pairs; reported only. **Haiku** reports too (about $0.1), flagged circular for B. **Latency, on the laptop, with a bar:** each writer's bit time (median and slowest 5%, the 95th percentile of its 60 bits) against bonsai's (Exp 26, D-110: median 3.5 s, 95th percentile 5.1 s for 20 bits, one thread per call) and Exp 24's single-call numbers (bonsai 5.2 s / 6.9 s; qwen3:4b-instruct 6.7 s / 8.2 s, three threads in one call, the older generator); **bar: the slowest 5% within the live per-bit budget (`generate.GENERATOR_TIMING[1]`, 12 s)**, so the show can use the writer. A and the trained writers are served by one Ollama, one at a time, with `ollama stop` between them, at the show's settings (`OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL=3`, context 8192).

**The win bar is NOT fixed. Proposal, to be confirmed by the user before `eval vote`, after the re-rank's self-agreement is known.** `eval vote` refuses to start until the user has run `eval bar --wins N`, and the bar cannot change once a vote exists. Power for 15 pairs (exact binomial; ties excluded: n is the number of decided pairs, the wins stay as given; one comparison):

| Bar (wins of 15 decided) | Coin flip (no skill) | Truly wins 65% | 70% | 75% |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| at least 9 | 30.4% | 75.5% | 86.9% | 94.3% |
| **at least 10 (proposed)** | **15.1%** | 56.4% | 72.2% | 85.2% |
| at least 11 | 5.9% | 35.2% | 51.5% | 68.6% |
| at least 12 | 1.8% | 17.3% | 29.7% | 46.1% |

With ties the same bar of 10 wins gets harder: of 14 decided, a coin flip passes 9.0% and a 70% writer 58%; of 12, 1.9% and 25%. B and C are two comparisons against the same A, so the chance that either meets "at least 10" by luck is **27.9%** (11.5% at "at least 11"). **Proposed bar: at least 10 wins of the 15 decided pairs in a comparison** (a coin flip passes 15% of the time; a writer that truly wins 70% passes 72%). It is a bar a lucky writer clears one time in seven, and 15 pairs cannot separate a 60% writer from a coin at all: a small real gain is invisible at this size, and a "not met" says "not shown", not "not better". The user may prefer 11 (6% false pass per comparison, 12% for either, 52% power at 70%). **Why wait for the re-rank:** the re-rank's self-agreement is a ceiling on how often any writer can beat A in this user's votes; if the user agrees with themselves about 70% of the time, a true 75% rate is not reachable and a bar of 10 is stricter than it looks. Judgment call, not measured. The same table is printed by `python -m sim.dpo_pairs eval power` and pinned by tests.

**Options and trade-offs.**
| Option | For | Against | Chosen |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) Stop: ship the two negative results (D-122's rule) | Honest; no cost | No SFT or DPO to show for the course's main deliverable | No (the user's call) |
| (b) DPO on Haiku's labels (writer B) | Thousands of pairs at about $1; on-policy for the base; the full RLAIF pipeline | Both judges failed to track the user, so what B learns is the judge's taste; reward-hacking risk; Haiku's reading of B is circular | **Yes** |
| (c) DPO on the user's 120 pairs (writer C) | The only labels that are a person's choices | 120 is small; bonsai's threads are off-policy for the qwen3 base; about 45 optimizer steps | **Yes** |
| (d) Rank more pairs for a bigger C | More signal from the right source | 12 to 16 hours for 120; no time | No |
| (e) Vote B against C too | Shows which route is better | Needs a third of the 30 votes each; 10 pairs per comparison cannot decide anything | No: Opus judges it, reported only |
| (f) Judge-only evaluation (Opus) | Hundreds of comparisons, cheap | Opus agrees with Haiku, not with the user; it cannot be the measure | No: reported only |

**What is not controlled.** The tonight half shares one day's headlines with training. The user's votes are a single person's, 15 pairs per comparison. The eval compares the first clean thread of a bit, not the thread the show's selector would play. Quantisation to Q4_K_M and the Ollama template may change a trained writer's behaviour a little from the fp16 model that was trained (the stock `qwen3:4b-instruct` is also Q4_K_M, so A and the trained writers are comparable). The sample size cannot detect a small real gain. The user's self-agreement (the ceiling) may not be known when the bar is set.

**If the bar is met / not met.** Met by a writer: it becomes the candidate for the live default (`JOKE_WRITER_MODEL`, a one-line change in a later step of this plan, after its latency bar also holds), and the report says what it was trained toward. Not met by both: the live default stays bonsai-2:4b, and the report says the trained writers are not shown better by 15 blind pairs. D-124 names bonsai as the live default until this decides.

**Commands (the user runs these; the PR runs none).** In order, with time and cost, in `explorer/README.md` ("Training the writer") and the PR body.

## <a id="d-124"></a>D-124 · 2026-11-27 — The training base, and future live writer, is qwen3:4b-instruct (Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507), not bonsai-2:4b (user)
Decided by the user, 2026-11-27, with D-123. It changes D-107's "bonsai is the live writer and the SFT/DPO base" for the base only: **the live default stays `bonsai-2:4b` until Exp 38 decides, then the winning qwen3 writer goes live** (a one-line change, in the same plan, after the user's eval).

**Why bonsai cannot be trained on Explorer.** Research (2026-11-27, not run; nothing was downloaded):
- Prism ML publishes Ternary-Bonsai-2-4B only as packed, rotated ternary weights in GGUF ([model page](https://huggingface.co/prism-ml/Ternary-Bonsai-2-4B-gguf)); there is **no fp16 checkpoint** to train.
- Its architecture is `qwen3_5`, which needs **transformers 5.2 or newer** ([transformers qwen3_5 docs](https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/model_doc/qwen3_5)). Explorer's stack is `torch==2.5.1+cu121` on a V100 whose driver supports CUDA up to 12.3, and transformers 5.x needs a newer torch than that (explorer/README.md). Neither can be changed by a course user.
- `qwen3` is supported by transformers 4.57.6, which is what the venv runs; the merged model converts back to GGUF Q4_K_M with llama.cpp (about 2.5 GB, the format of today's local `qwen3:4b-instruct`), so it serves on the laptop through Ollama exactly like the base does.

**What Exp 24 said about it (D-107, `reports/metrics/model_comparison.json`).** qwen3:4b-instruct passed every eligibility bar: 60 of 60 attempts valid, p50 6.7 s, p95 8.2 s (bonsai 5.2 s and 6.9 s), line pass 87% (bonsai 89%), playable 87% (bonsai 92%), mean rank 2.17 with a 95% interval of 1.90 to 2.43, **exactly tied with bonsai's 2.17**; head to head qwen was 0.13 ranks ahead, interval -0.20 to 0.47, inconclusive. D-107 picked bonsai on speed and playability, not on funniness. So the new base is not known to be worse; it is known to be slower (about 1.5 s a bit in that run, under the one-call generator of the time). **Not measured:** its time under D-110's one-thread-per-call generator (bonsai's 3.5 s median there); Exp 38's latency measure does that for A, B and C.

**Options.**
| Option | For | Against | Chosen |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) qwen3:4b-instruct (Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507) | fp16 weights exist; supported by transformers 4.57.6; already local and passed Exp 24; non-thinking (no `enable_thinking` question); GGUF Q4_K_M round trip | Slower than bonsai in Exp 24; Ollama and Hugging Face templates differ by a blank line (handled, D-123) | **Yes** |
| (b) bonsai-2:4b | The live writer; fastest | No fp16 checkpoint; needs transformers 5.2 or newer; cannot run on Explorer | No |
| (c) Train bonsai from its GGUF by dequantising | Keeps the model | Packed, rotated ternary weights are not a normal training start; no supported path; unverified | No |
| (d) Another open model (a Qwen3 4B Thinking, Gemma) | Might be funnier | Not tested in Exp 24 (gemma4:12b timed out there); each would need its own screen and its own template check | No |
| (e) Keep bonsai and train nothing | No new risk | No SFT or DPO | No (D-123) |

**Judgment calls, not measured.** That qwen3:4b-instruct's fp16 model trained with LoRA then quantised to Q4_K_M behaves like the Ollama stock quantisation of the base; that the live budget holds for it under one thread per call; that its tie with bonsai on funniness in Exp 24 holds for the prompts of Exp 38.

**What changes in the repo.** `generate.writer_client(model)` and the pool tools' `--writer` flag take the model; nothing in the show changes: `JOKE_WRITER_MODEL` still decides the live writer.
